An operator system approach to self-testing
Jason Crann 1, Ivan G. Todorov 2, Lyudmila Turowska 3

Abstract
. We develop a general framework for self-testing, in which bipartite correlations are described by states on the commuting tensor product of a pair of operator systems. We propose a definition of a local isometry between bipartite quantum systems in the commuting operator model, and define self-testing and abstract self-testing in the latter generality. We show that self-tests are in the general case always abstract self-tests and that, in some cases, the converse is also true. We apply our framework in a variety of instances, including to correlations with quantum inputs and outputs, quantum commuting correlations for the CHSH game, synchronous correlations, contextuality scenarios, quantum colourings and Schur quantum channels.



1.
Introduction

About two decades ago, a remarkable feature of quantum computation was uncovered
in
[
39
]
: quantum devices can be certified
either by a user or by an independent third party by only observing
their outputs when completing specific quantum computational tasks.
This feature became known as
self-testing
, and was intensively studied in the
subsequent years, in particular in connection with device-independent quantum
cryptography (see e.g.
[
7
,
8
,
48
,
49
]
).
The notion was used in the celebrated solution of
the Tsirelson Problem in
[
27
]
, and
a number of recent advances on the topic, from a variety of perspectives, has been
achieved in
[
11
,
15
,
38
,
40
,
44
,
58
]
.
A survey of the concept and associated results can be found in
[
52
]
.

In its simplest form, namely, the (bipartite) Bell scenario, self-testing rests on the correlation between the behaviours of two non-communicating
parties, Alice and Bob, performing a quantum experiment.
Each of the two parties holds a quantum system, modeled by two finite dimensional
Hilbert spaces
H_{A}
and
H_{B}
, and share an entangled state,
modeled by a unit vector
\xi
in the tensor product
H_{A}\otimes H_{B}
.
Alice (resp. Bob) has access to measurement devices, indexed by
a finite set
X
(resp.
Y
), modeled by
POVM’s, say
(E_{x,a})_{a\in A}
,
x\in X
,
(resp.
(F_{y,b})_{b\in B}
,
y\in Y
), where
A
(resp.
B
)
is the set of outputs for Alice (resp. Bob).
The tuple
M=(H_{A},H_{B},(E_{x,a})_{a\in A},(F_{y,b})_{b\in B},\xi)

yields a
correlation
, that is, a family
p_{M}
of conditional
probability distributions over
A\times B
, given by

(1)

p_{M}(a,b|x,y)=\langle(E_{x,a}\otimes F_{y,b})\xi,\xi\rangle,\ \ \ x\in X,y\in Y,a\in A,b\in B.

We say that the tuple
M
is a model of
p_{M}
;
naturally, different models may yield the same correlation
p_{M}
.
In the Bell scenario, self-testing consists in the fact that, in some cases, the correlation
p_{M}

determines uniquely (up to a natural equivalence) the model
M
.
Setups for self-testing, distinct from the Bell scenario, have also been
considered; for example, it was shown in
[
4
]
that
probabilistic assignments of some contextuality scenarios can be
self-tested, thus enlarging the capabilities of self-testing
beyond classical bipartite experiments.

Recent operator algebraic approaches to self-testing in the
Bell scenario were made in
[
38
]
,
[
44
]
and
[
58
]
.
They rely on the fact that, given the index sets
X
and
A
for Alice,
there exist an operator system
\mathcal{S}_{X,A}

(resp. a C*-algebra
\mathcal{A}_{X,A}
) that
is universal for families of POVM’s
(E_{x,a})_{a\in A}
,
indexed over
X
, in that the latter
completely determine the unital completely positive maps
(resp. unital *-homomorphisms) on the former.
This led to an abstract view on self-testing, developed in

[
44
]
and pursued further in
[
58
]
, which resides in
the states of the commuting tensor product
\mathcal{S}_{X,A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{Y,B}

[
31
]
admitting a unique extension to a state on the
maximal tensor product
\mathcal{A}_{X,A}\otimes_{\max}\mathcal{A}_{Y,B}
.
It was shown in
[
44
]
that, in some cases, self-testing and abstract
self-testing coincide.

In a parallel development, the
commuting operator model

of quantum mechanics has been gaining importance over
the past decade
as a genuinely different (due to
[
27
]
) and useful model
in quantum information theory; see, for example,
[
17
,
18
,
46
,
47
]
.
Abstract self-testing was studied in the commuting operator model
in
[
44
]
, but no advances have so far been made on providing an
operational definition of self-testing in the latter context.

On the other hand,
in connections with non-local games with quantum inputs and quantum outputs,
no-signalling correlations were extended to the quantum setting in

[
55
]
, and further used as strategies for such games,
notably for games arising from quantum graphs, in
[
9
]

and
[
10
]
. The question of a
suitable framework for self-testing of quantum-to-quantum, or
classical-to-quantum, correlations has been, however, open.

The purpose of the present work is to fill both of these gaps.
We provide a far-reaching framework for self-testing, which includes
as a special case self-testing in the Bell scenario for
classical bipartitie no-signalling correlations, as well as
known instances of self-testing for a single contextuality scenario,
simultaneously developing an operational concept of self-testing
in the commuting operator framework.
Our setup includes self-testing of all correlation types of current interest,
and is developed in a general case, where the correlations between Alice and Bob
arise from an arbitrary pair of operator systems.
We apply our framework in a variety of
new instances: quantum commuting self-testing for the CHSH game,
self-testing of classical correlations arising from
representations of the Clifford relations, of synchronous correlations
of quantum type, of probabilistic assignments over a pair of
contextuality scenarios, of perfect strategies for quantum colourings
of classical complete graphs, as well as of quantum channels
arising from Schur multipliers.

We describe the content of the paper in more detail.
In self-testing of no-signalling correlations of quantum type,
a fundamental role for the identification
of the uniqueness of the model
M
(see (
1
))
is played by
local isometries
;
in this case the latter have the form
V_{A}\otimes V_{B}
, where
V_{A}:H_{A}\to K_{A}
and

V_{B}:H_{B}\to K_{B}
are isometries between the Alice’s and Bob’s systems,
respectively.
Local isometries allow to push forward correlation models
via operations on each of the systems of Alice and Bob, separately.
In the commuting operator model, the tensor splitting

H_{A}\otimes H_{B}
of the joint quantum system is not available; instead,
the latter is modeled by a single Hilbert space
H
.
In Section
2
we therefore develop a concept of
a local isometry between quantum commuting bipartite systems, which leads to a natural relation of dominance of one system by another.
Along with establishing some basic properties, related to composing and
tensoring, we show in Theorem
2.9
that the dominance relation is a
pre-order.

In Section
3
, we propose a notion of a model
of a correlation for quantum commuting systems.
The quantum commuting correlations between Alice and Bob are represented
via states on the commuting tensor product
\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B}
of
operator systems
\mathcal{S}_{A}
and
\mathcal{S}_{B}
, where the former captures
the local degrees of freedom of Alice, while the latter – those of Bob.
A quantum commuting model over the pair
(\mathcal{S}_{A},\mathcal{S}_{B})

is defined as a tuple

S=(_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{B}},\varphi_{A},\varphi_{B},\xi)
,
where
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are the von Neumann algebras of local observables
for Alice and Bob, respectively, acting on a Hilbert space
H
,

\xi\in H
is a pure state, and

\varphi_{A}:\mathcal{S}_{A}\to\mathcal{B}(H)
and
\varphi_{B}:\mathcal{S}_{B}\to\mathcal{B}(H)

are unital completely positive maps with commuting ranges that are contained in
the corresponding observable algebras.
The model
S
determines a correlation
f_{S}
, given by

(2)

f_{S}(u)=\langle(\varphi_{A}\cdot\varphi_{B})(u)\xi,\xi\rangle,\ \ \ u\in\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B}.

Local isometries now allow to define a
local dilation
relation

S\preceq\tilde{S}
between models
S
and
\tilde{S}

(of the same correlation); we show that the latter is a pre-order.
In fact, it is natural to work in what appears to be a greater generality
of the
approximate local dilation
order
S\preceq_{\rm a}\tilde{S}
,
for which we require the existence of a sequence of local isometries that
attain
\tilde{S}
from
S
in the limit, and
which continues to imply that
f_{S}=f_{\tilde{S}}
.
(Approximate) local dilations between models lead
naturally to the notion of (weak) self-testing of a given
quantum commuting correlation by requiring
the existence of a maximal (with respect to the
(approximate) local dilation pre-order) model for the given correlation.
The main result of Section
3
is Theorem
3.13
, where we
show that, under some additional hypotheses, if the correlation
f
is a
weak self-test then it is an abstract self-test;
the result extends
[
44
, Proposition 4.10]
to the commuting operator framework.

In Section
4
, we study further abstract self-tests,
providing a characterisation thereof in the general case.
In Theorem
4.3
, we extend
[
44
, Theorem 3.5]
,
showing that self-testing is equivalent to abstract self-testing
for correlations arising from tensor products
\varphi_{A}\otimes\varphi_{B}

in the place of products
\varphi_{A}\cdot\varphi_{B}
in (
2
),
in the case where the observable algebras of Alice and Bob are of type I.

Section
5
is dedicated to applications and examples.
In their majority, they are concerned with
finitary
quantum systems,
that is, based on a pair of finite dimensional operator systems.
In Subsection
5.1
, we show how the proposed framework includes
self-testing of QNS correlations introduced in
[
55
]
.
Subsection
5.2
places the self-testing of
POVM correlations
[
44
]
in our setup, and explores the
relation between the latter and QNS self-testing. En route,
we record some new observations about the relation between
the universal operator systems for POVM self-testing and
QNS self-testing. Example
5.6
demonstrates that
self-tests for classical no-signalling correlations cannot be
automatically lifted to self-tests for quantum no-signalling correlations.
Subsection
5.3
shows that the optimal quantum strategy
for the CHSH game persists in being a self-test within the class of
quantum commuting correlations.

In Subsection
5.4
, we continue the study of self-testing
for no-signalling correlations of quantum type.
We single out a class such correlations (which we call Clifford correlations),
arising from representations of the Clifford relations, and
show that synchronous Clifford correlations are abstract self-tests.
We employ the NPA hierarchy
[
42
]
, showing that the
latter fact leads to self-tests among the correlations with
constraints on their order-two moments.

Subsection
5.5
shows how our framework can host
self-testing for a pair of contextuality scenarios
[
1
]
.
We note that the framework of contextuality scenarios is designed to include as a special case
no-signalling correlations; however, no-signalling is usually studied alongside
contextuality.
We show that, even in the case where one of the scenarios
consists of a single outcome,
this setting may be non-trivial, by casting
[
4
, Main Theorem]

in our language.

In Subection
5.6
, we show one yet new special case hosted within
our framework, namely, classical-to-quantum no-signalling correlations.
We show that perfect strategies for the quantum colouring game of classical
complete graphs are self-tests.
Finally, Subsection
5.7
provides self-testing examples among
Schur quantum channels.

The relation between abstract self-testing – requiring a unique extension of a state to a tensor product of C*-covers – and self-testing – defined through the existence of a maximal dilation – is reminiscent of the relation between the unique extension property (UEP) for unital completely positive maps on operator systems and maximality in dilation order
[
3
]
, wherein C*-envelopes play a crucial role. In fact, this similarity was one of our motivations for developing an operator system approach to self-testing. In order to elucidate this similarity, in Section
6
we show that many of the special
cases studied in the literature as well as our new self-testing examples fall in the class of states
that can be factored through the tensor product of C*-envelopes of the
ground operator systems. We also show that every stochastic operator
matrix admits a unitary dilation, allowing us to identify the
C*-envelope of the universal operator system of QNS correlations. We leave a deeper investigation of the connections with UEP to future work.

In Section
7
, we comment on some questions
arising from our work.

Notation.

We let
\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y}
be the algebraic tensor product of
vector spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, unless the latter are Hilbert spaces,
in which case the notation will be reserved for their Hilbertian tensor product.
Given Hilbert spaces
H
and
K
, we let
\mathcal{B}(H,K)
be the
(Banach) space of all bounded linear operators from
H
into
K
;
we set
\mathcal{B}(H)=\mathcal{B}(H,H)
.
If
\mathcal{A}
is a unital C*-algebra, we write
1_{\mathcal{A}}
for its unit, and let

1_{H}=I_{H}=1_{\mathcal{B}(H)}
.
The opposite C*-algebra
\mathcal{A}^{o}
of
\mathcal{A}
has the same
set-theoretic, linear and involutive structure as
\mathcal{A}
and, writing its elements as
a^{o}
, where
a\in\mathcal{A}
, its multiplication is
given by letting
a_{1}^{o}a_{2}^{o}=(a_{2}a_{1})^{o}
,
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathcal{A}
.
The maximal (resp. minimal) tesnor product of C*-algebras
\mathcal{A}
and

\mathcal{B}
will be denoted by
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\max}\mathcal{B}

(resp.
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\min}\mathcal{B}
). If
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are von Neumann algebras,
their spatial weak* closed tensor product will be denoted by

\mathcal{A}\bar{\otimes}\mathcal{B}
.

For a finite set
X
, we let
\{e_{x}\}_{x\in X}
denote the canonical
orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{C}^{X}
. We write
M_{X}
for the algebra of all

X\times X
complex matrices, and let
\{\epsilon_{x,x^{\prime}}\}_{x,x^{\prime}\in X}

be the canonical matrix unit system in
M_{X}
. We let
{\rm Tr}

(resp.
{\rm tr}
) be the trace (resp. normalised trace) on
M_{X}
; we write

{\rm Tr}_{|X|}
(resp.
{\rm tr}_{|X|}
)
to improve clarity as needed.

2.
Local isometries

In this section, we define a notion of local isometry
in the commuting operator framework of quantum mechanics and
establish some of its properties that will be used in the sequel.
We first recall the tensor product model of composite quantum systems;
it serves as
a motivating example which we aim to generalise.

Let
H_{A}
,
K_{A}
,
H_{B}
and
K_{B}
be Hilbert spaces;
they give rise to two (bipartite)
quantum mechanical systems with observable
algebras

\mathcal{B}(H_{A}\otimes H_{B})=\mathcal{B}(H_{A})\bar{\otimes}\mathcal{B}(H_{B})
and

\mathcal{B}(K_{A}\otimes K_{B})=\mathcal{B}(K_{A})\bar{\otimes}\mathcal{B}(K_{B})
, respectively.
An isometry
V:H_{A}\otimes H_{B}\to K_{A}\otimes K_{B}
is called

local
if

V=V_{A}\otimes V_{B}
for some isometries

V_{A}:H_{A}\to K_{A}
and
V_{B}:H_{B}\to K_{B}
.
We may view
V_{A}\otimes V_{B}
as a composition
in two ways,
by introducing the
intermediate Hilbert spaces
H_{A}\otimes K_{B}
and
K_{A}\otimes H_{B}
and writing

V_{A}\otimes V_{B}=(V_{A}\otimes I_{K_{B}})\circ(I_{H_{A}}\otimes V_{B})=(I_{K_{A}}\otimes V_{B})\circ(V_{A}\otimes{I_{H_{B}}});

in other words, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{A}\otimes H_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{A}\otimes K_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{A}\otimes H_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{A}\otimes K_{B}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{A}\otimes I_{H_{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{H_{A}}\otimes V_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{A}\otimes I_{K_{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{K_{A}}\otimes V_{B}}

Towards formulating a suitable extension
of the framework described in the previous paragraph,
we fix von Neumann algebras
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
.
A
bipartite quantum system
over the pair
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})

is a pair
(H,\pi)
, where

H
is a Hilbert space and

\pi:\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\max}\mathcal{B}^{o}\to\mathcal{B}(H)
is
a unital
*
-representation, normal in each of the two variables (equivalently,
\pi
is a unital representation of the binormal tensor product
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\rm bin}\mathcal{B}^{o}

[
23
]
).
We will often write
\pi=\pi_{H}
if we want to emphasise the
underlying Hilbert space.
We let
\pi_{H}(a)=\pi(a\otimes 1)
,
a\in\mathcal{A}
, and

\pi_{H}(b^{o})=\pi(1\otimes b^{o})
,
b^{o}\in\mathcal{B}^{o}
.
We set

a\cdot\xi:=\pi_{H}(a)\xi\ \mbox{ and }\ \xi\cdot b:=\pi_{H}(b^{o})\xi,\ \ \ a\in\mathcal{A},\ b\in\mathcal{B},\ \xi\in H;

thus, a bipartite system over
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is, equivalently,
a normal Hilbertian
\mathcal{A}
-
\mathcal{B}
-bimodule
[
54
, §IX.3]
.
For clarity, we will use the notation
{}_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{B}}
in the place of
H
,
and, by abuse of notation,
will refer to
{}_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{B}}
as a biparite quantum system.
Heuristically, a bipartite quantum system
{}_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{B}}
is
a joint quantum system of parties
Alice and Bob; the algebra
\mathcal{A}
represents Alice’s part of the system
(namely, the observables accessible to Alice), while
the algebra
\mathcal{B}
– Bob’s part of the system
(namely, the observables accessible to Bob).

Remark 2.1
.

If
{}_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{B}}
and
{}_{\mathcal{C}}K_{\mathcal{D}}
are biparite quantum systems then
the tensor product
H\otimes K
is a bipartite quantum system
over the pair
(\mathcal{A}\bar{\otimes}\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}\bar{\otimes}\mathcal{D})
in a canonical fashion.
We write
H\otimes K=\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{B}}\otimes\mbox{}_{\mathcal{C}}K_{\mathcal{D}}
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}_{i}
be von Neumann algebras,
i=1,2
,
and
{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}H_{\mathcal{B}}
,
{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}K_{\mathcal{B}}
,
{}_{\mathcal{A}}\tilde{H}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}
and

{}_{\mathcal{A}}\tilde{K}_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}
be biparite quantum systems.

(i)

An operator
T\in\mathcal{B}(H,K)
is called
\mathcal{A}_{1}
-
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-local if

(i’)

T(\xi\cdot b)=(T\xi)\cdot b
,
\xi\in H
,
b\in\mathcal{B}
, and

(i”)

T\pi_{H}(\mathcal{A}_{1})T^{*}\subseteq\pi_{K}(\mathcal{A}_{2})
.

(ii)

An operator
\tilde{T}\in\mathcal{B}(\tilde{H},\tilde{K})
is called
\mathcal{B}_{1}
-
\mathcal{B}_{2}
-local if

(ii’)

\tilde{T}(a\cdot\tilde{\xi})=a\cdot(\tilde{T}\tilde{\xi})
,

\tilde{\xi}\in\tilde{H}
,
a\in\mathcal{A}
, and

(ii”)

T\pi_{\tilde{H}}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{o})T^{*}\subseteq\pi_{\tilde{K}}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{o})
.

If no confusion arises,
\mathcal{A}_{1}
-
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-local
(resp.
\mathcal{B}_{1}
-
\mathcal{B}_{2}
-local) operators will be simply referred to
as
\mathcal{A}
-local
(resp.
\mathcal{B}
-local
).
We note that the
\mathcal{B}
-module condition (i’) in Definition
2.2

is equivalent to the operator

T:\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}H_{\mathcal{B}}\to\textbf{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}K_{\mathcal{B}}

being an intertwiner between the representations
\pi_{H}
and
\pi_{K}
of
\mathcal{B}
.
Intuitively, such an intertwiner
is
\mathcal{A}
-local if, in addition, the conjugation by
T
leaves
Alice’s algebras of observables globally invariant. Such module and invariance conditions are common features among notions of local operations in algebraic quantum theory (see e.g.
[
16
,
17
,
33
,
36
,
57
]
).

Definition 2.3
.

Let

{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791ptH_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}
and

{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791ptK_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}
be bipartite quantum systems over
(\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i})
,
i=1,2
.
An operator
T:\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791ptH_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}\to\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791ptK_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}
is
called local if there exist
bipartite quantum systems

{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791ptL_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}
and
{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791pt\widetilde{L}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}
,
\mathcal{A}_{1}
-
\mathcal{A}_{2}
local maps

T_{1,1}:\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791ptH_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}\to\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791pt\widetilde{L}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}\ \mbox{ and }\ T_{2,2}:\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791ptL_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}\to\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791ptK_{\mathcal{B}_{2}},

and
\mathcal{B}_{1}
-
\mathcal{B}_{2}
local maps

T_{1,2}:\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791ptH_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}\to\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791ptL_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}\ \mbox{ and }\ T_{2,1}=\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791pt\widetilde{L}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}\to\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791ptK_{\mathcal{B}_{2}},

for which the diagram

(3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791ptH_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791ptL_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791pt\widetilde{L}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791ptK_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{1,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{1,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{2,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{2,1}}

commutes, and

T=T_{2,2}\circ T_{1,2}\ (=T_{2,1}\circ T_{1,1})
. When all the local maps
T_{i,j}
can be chosen to be
isometric, we say that
T
is a local isometry. In this case, we write
{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791ptH_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}\leq\,_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791ptK_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}
.

Henceforth, when discussing local isometries we will stick with the convention that the “veritcal maps”
T_{1,1}
and
T_{2,2}
are
\mathcal{A}
-local and the “horizontal maps” are
\mathcal{B}
-local.

Remark 2.4
.

(i)

Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
be commuting von Neumann algebras acting on a Hilbert space
H
. Then we can view
H=_{\mathcal{A}}\hskip-1.13791ptH_{\mathcal{B}^{o}}
as an
\mathcal{A}
-
\mathcal{B}^{o}
-bimodule in the canonical fashion. Let
u\in\mathcal{A}
and
v\in\mathcal{B}
be isometries. Then the product

uv:\,_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{B}^{o}}\to\,_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{B}^{o}}
is a local isometry in the sense of Definition
2.3
, with canonical factorisations.

(ii)

In the context of item (i), assume that

{}_{\mathcal{A}}\hskip-1.13791ptH_{\mathcal{B}}\leq\,_{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}\hskip-1.13791pt\tilde{H}_{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}}
,
implemented by isometries
T_{i,j}
,
i,j=1,2
, as in (
3
).
Writing
{}_{\mathcal{A}}\hskip-1.13791ptL_{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}}
for the
upper right corner of (
3
), we have that there
exists a normal *-representation
\sigma:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}(L)

and a normal *-representation
\rho:\sigma(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{B}(\tilde{H})
,
such that
{\rm ran}(\rho)\subseteq\pi_{\tilde{H}}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}})
, and

(4)

T_{1,2}a=\sigma(a)T_{1,2}\ \mbox{ and }\ T_{2,2}\sigma(a)=\rho(\sigma(a))T_{2,2},\ \ \ a\in\mathcal{A}.

Indeed, the existence of
\sigma
and the first relation in (
4
)
are a restatement of condition (ii’) in Definition
2.2
,
while
\rho
can be defined by letting

\rho(\sigma(a))=T_{2,2}\sigma(a)T_{2,2}^{*}
,
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

Example 2.5
.

For a Hilbert space
H
, let
\bar{H}
be its dual Banach space,
and let
\partial:H\to\bar{H}
be the conjugate-linear isometry,
given by
\partial(\xi)(\eta)=\langle\eta,\xi\rangle
,
\xi,\eta\in H
.
We write
\bar{\xi}=\partial(\xi)
,
\xi\in H
.
If
K
is a(nother) Hilbert space, let

\mathcal{S}_{2}(\bar{K},H)
be the Hilbert
space of all Hilbert-Schmidt operators from
\bar{K}
into
H
, and

\theta:H\otimes K\to\mathcal{S}_{2}(\bar{K},H)
be the unitary operator,
given by

\theta(\xi\otimes\eta)(\bar{\zeta})=\langle\eta,\zeta\rangle\xi
.

Let
H_{A}
,
K_{A}
,
H_{B}
and
K_{B}
be Hilbert spaces.
We note the canonical identification
\mathcal{B}(H_{B})^{o}=\mathcal{B}(\bar{H}_{B})
,
where an element
b^{o}\in\mathcal{B}(H_{B})^{o}
is identified
with the dual operator
\bar{b}:\bar{H}_{B}\to\bar{H}_{B}

of
b\in\mathcal{B}(H_{B})
.
Consider the normal *-representation

\pi_{H_{A}\otimes H_{B}}:\mathcal{B}(H_{A})\otimes_{\max}\mathcal{B}(H_{B})^{o}\to\mathcal{B}(H_{A}\otimes H_{B})
, given by

\pi_{H}(a)(\xi)=\theta^{-1}(a\theta(\xi)),\ \ \ \pi_{H}(b^{o})(\xi)=\theta^{-1}(\theta(\xi)\bar{b}),

where
a\in\mathcal{B}(H_{A}),b^{o}\in\mathcal{B}(H_{B})^{o}
and
\xi\in H_{A}\otimes H_{B}
.
We write
b^{\rm t}
for the operator on
H_{B}
for which

\pi_{H}(b^{o})=I_{H_{A}}\otimes b^{\rm t}
,
b\in\mathcal{B}(H_{B})
.
The *-representation
\pi_{H_{A}\otimes H_{B}}

gives rise to a bipartite quantum system

{}_{\mathcal{B}(H_{A})}(H_{A}\otimes H_{B})_{\mathcal{B}(H_{B})^{o}}
; we similarly obtain
a bipartite quantum system

{}_{\mathcal{B}(K_{A})}(K_{A}\otimes K_{B})_{\mathcal{B}(K_{B})^{o}}
.
Quantum systems of the latter form will be called

quantum spacial systems
.

Suppose that
V_{A}:H_{A}\to K_{A}
and
V_{B}:H_{B}\to K_{B}
are isometries.
Let
L=H_{A}\otimes K_{B}
and
\widetilde{L}=K_{A}\otimes H_{B}
,
and consider the bipartite quantum systems

{}_{\mathcal{B}(H_{A})}{L}_{B(K_{B})^{o}}
and

{}_{\mathcal{B}(K_{A})}\widetilde{L}_{\mathcal{B}(H_{B})^{o}}
,
arising as described in the previous paragraph.
Setting

T_{1,1}=V_{A}\otimes I_{H_{B}}
,

T_{1,2}=I_{H_{A}}\otimes V_{B}
,
T_{2,1}=I_{K_{A}}\otimes V_{B}
and

T_{2,2}=V_{A}\otimes I_{K_{B}}
, we
obtain a commutative diagram

(5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{B}(H_{A})}(H_{A}\otimes H_{B})_{\mathcal{B}(H_{B})^{o}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{B}(H_{A})}L_{B(K_{B})^{o}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{B}(K_{A})}\widetilde{L}_{\mathcal{B}(H_{B})^{o}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{B}(K_{A})}(K_{A}\otimes K_{B})_{\mathcal{B}(K_{B})^{o}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{1,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{1,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{2,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{2,1}}

for which
V_{A}\otimes V_{B}=T_{2,2}T_{1,2}=T_{2,1}T_{1,1}
.
We will call the local isometries of the latter form
split
.

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
H_{A}
,
K_{A}
,
H_{B}
and
K_{B}
be Hilbert spaces.
Every local isometry from
{}_{\mathcal{B}(H_{A})}(H_{A}\otimes H_{B})_{\mathcal{B}(H_{B})^{o}}
to
{}_{\mathcal{B}(K_{A})}(K_{A}\otimes K_{B})_{\mathcal{B}(K_{B})^{o}}

is split.

Proof.

Suppose that

V:H_{A}\otimes H_{B}\to K_{A}\otimes K_{B}
is a local isometry in the sense of Definition
2.3
, and let

{}_{\mathcal{B}(H_{A})}L_{B(K_{B})^{o}}
and
{}_{\mathcal{B}(K_{A})}\widetilde{L}_{\mathcal{B}(H_{B})^{o}}

be bipartite quantum systems, while

T_{1,1},T_{1,2},T_{2,1}
and
T_{2,2}
are local isometries
in the diagram (
5
),
such that
V=T_{2,2}T_{1,2}=T_{2,1}T_{1,1}
.
Up to unitary conjugations, we may assume that

L=H_{A}\otimes K_{B}\otimes L_{0}
and
\widetilde{L}=K_{A}\otimes H_{B}\otimes\widetilde{L}_{0}

for some Hilbert spaces
L_{0}
and
\widetilde{L}_{0}
,
so that

\pi_{L}(a)=a\otimes I_{K_{B}}\otimes I_{L_{0}}\ \mbox{ and }\ \pi_{L}(b^{o})=I_{H_{A}}\otimes b^{\rm t}\otimes I_{L_{0}}^{\rm t},

for
a\in\mathcal{B}(H_{A})
and
b\in\mathcal{B}(K_{B})
,
and

\pi_{\widetilde{L}}(a)=a\otimes I_{H_{B}}\otimes I_{\widetilde{L}_{0}}\ \mbox{ and }\ \pi_{\widetilde{L}}(b^{o})=I_{K_{A}}\otimes b^{\rm t}\otimes I_{\widetilde{L}_{0}}^{\rm t},

for
a\in\mathcal{B}(K_{A})
and
b\in\mathcal{B}(H_{B})
.
Condition (ii’) in Definition
2.2
implies that, for every

h_{1},h_{2}\in H_{A}
,
\xi\in H_{B}
,
\eta\in K_{B}\otimes L_{0}
and

a\in\mathcal{B}(H_{A})
, we have

\langle T_{1,2}(ah_{1}\otimes\xi),h_{2}\otimes\eta\rangle=\langle T_{1,2}(h_{1}\otimes\xi)),a^{*}h_{2}\otimes\eta\rangle.

It follows that

(\id_{H_{A}}\otimes\omega_{\xi,\eta})(T_{1,2})\in\mathcal{B}(H_{A})^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}I_{H_{A}}.

Letting
\lambda_{\xi,\eta}\in\mathbb{C}
with

(\id_{H_{A}}\otimes\omega_{\xi,\eta})(T_{1,2})=\lambda_{\xi,\eta}I_{H_{A}}
,
it is straightforward to verify that the map

(\xi,\eta)\to\lambda_{\xi,\eta}
is conjugate-linear and
bounded, therefore yielding a bounded operator

W_{B}:H_{B}\to K_{B}\otimes L_{0}
, such that

\lambda_{\xi,\eta}=\langle W_{B}\xi,\eta\rangle,\ \ \ \xi\in H_{B},\eta\in K_{B}\otimes L_{0}.

Thus
T_{1,2}=I_{H_{A}}\otimes W_{B}
and, since
T_{1,2}
is an
isometry, so is
W_{B}
.

Condition (ii”) in Definition
2.2
now reads

(I_{H_{A}}\otimes W_{B})(I_{H_{A}}\otimes\mathcal{B}(H_{B})^{o})(I_{H_{A}}\otimes W_{B}^{*})\subseteq I_{H_{A}}\otimes\mathcal{B}(K_{B})^{o}\otimes I_{L_{0}},

that is,

(6)

W_{B}\mathcal{B}(H_{B})^{o}W_{B}^{*}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(K_{B})^{o}\otimes I_{L_{0}}.

If
b^{o}\in\mathcal{B}(H_{B})^{o}
is a rank one operator, then
the left hand side in (
6
) is a rank one operator,
implying that
\dim L_{0}=1
.
Similarly,
\dim\widetilde{L}_{0}=1
, and there exist
isometries
\widetilde{W_{B}}:H_{B}\to K_{B}
,

W_{A}:H_{A}\to K_{A}
and

\widetilde{W_{A}}:H_{A}\to K_{A}
such that

T_{2,1}=I_{K_{A}}\otimes\widetilde{W_{B}},\ \ T_{1,1}=W_{A}\otimes I_{H_{B}},\ \ \textnormal{and}\ \ T_{2,2}=\widetilde{W_{A}}\otimes I_{K_{B}}.

It follows that

V=\widetilde{W_{A}}\otimes W_{B}=W_{A}\otimes\widetilde{W_{B}}
, implying
that
W_{A}=\widetilde{W_{A}}
and
W_{B}=\widetilde{W_{B}}
.
The proof is complete.
∎

Example 2.7
.

Let
{}_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{C}}
,
{}_{\mathcal{A}}\widetilde{H}_{\mathcal{C}}
,
{}_{\mathcal{C}}K_{\mathcal{B}}
and
{}_{\mathcal{C}}\widetilde{K}_{\mathcal{B}}
be bipartite quantum system, for some
von Neumann algebras
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
. Suppose that

V:\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{C}}\to\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}}\widetilde{H}_{\mathcal{C}}
is an

\mathcal{A}
-local isometry and
W:\mbox{}_{\mathcal{C}}K_{\mathcal{B}}\to\mbox{}_{\mathcal{C}}\widetilde{K}_{\mathcal{B}}
is a

\mathcal{B}
-local isometry.
Fix a faithful normal semi-finite weight
\varphi
on
\mathcal{C}
(or a faithful normal state if
\mathcal{C}
is
\sigma
-finite),
and let
\boxtimes
denote Connes’ fusion product of bimodules relative to
\varphi

[
54
, Definition IX.3.16]
.
Then the commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{C}}\boxtimes\mbox{}_{\mathcal{C}}K_{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{C}}\boxtimes\mbox{}_{\mathcal{C}}\widetilde{K}_{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}}\widetilde{H}_{\mathcal{C}}\boxtimes\mbox{}_{\mathcal{C}}K_{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}}\widetilde{H}_{\mathcal{C}}\boxtimes\mbox{}_{\mathcal{C}}\widetilde{K}_{\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\otimes I_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{H}\otimes W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\otimes I_{\widetilde{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{\widetilde{H}}\otimes W}

gives rise to a local isometry

V\boxtimes W:\ _{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{C}}\boxtimes\mbox{}_{\mathcal{C}}K_{\mathcal{B}}\to_{\mathcal{A}}\widetilde{H}_{\mathcal{C}}\boxtimes\mbox{}_{\mathcal{C}}\widetilde{K}_{\mathcal{B}}
.

Remark 2.8
.

(i) It is straightforward to see that, if

\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{B}
(resp.
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}_{i}
) are von Neumann algebras,

{}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}H^{(i)}_{\mathcal{B}}
(resp.
{}_{\mathcal{A}}K^{(i)}_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}
)
are biparite quantum systems,
i=1,2,3
, and

S_{1}\in\mathcal{B}(H^{(1)},H^{(2)})
and
S_{2}\in\mathcal{B}(H^{(2)},H^{(3)})

(resp.

T_{1}\in\mathcal{B}(K^{(1)},K^{(2)})
and
T_{2}\in\mathcal{B}(K^{(2)},K^{(3)})
)
are
\mathcal{A}
-local (resp.
\mathcal{B}
-local) operators, then the operator
S_{2}S_{1}

(resp.
T_{2}T_{1}
) is
\mathcal{A}
-local (resp.
\mathcal{B}
-local).

(ii)
Let
{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\hskip-1.42271ptH_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}
and

{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791ptK_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}

(resp.
{}_{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{1}}\hskip-1.42271pt\tilde{H}_{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{1}}
and

{}_{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791pt\tilde{K}_{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}
)
be bipartite quantum systems over
(\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i})

(resp.
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{i},\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i})
),
i=1,2
.
If
T:\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791ptH_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}\to\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791ptK_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}

and
\tilde{T}:\mbox{}_{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791pt\tilde{H}_{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{1}}\to\mbox{}_{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791pt\tilde{K}_{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}

are local maps (resp. isometries) then

T\otimes\tilde{T}:\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791ptH_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}\otimes\mbox{}_{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791pt\tilde{H}_{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{1}}\to\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791ptK_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}\otimes\mbox{}_{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791pt\tilde{K}_{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}

is a local map (resp. isometry).
Indeed, suppose that

T=T_{2,2}\circ T_{1,2}=T_{2,1}\circ T_{1,1}
and

\tilde{T}=\tilde{T}_{2,2}\circ\tilde{T}_{1,2}=\tilde{T}_{2,1}\circ\tilde{T}_{1,1}
as in diagram (
3
).
Setting
\hat{T}_{i,j}=T_{i,j}\otimes\tilde{T}_{i,j}
, we witness the
locality of
T\otimes\tilde{T}
through the identity

T\otimes\tilde{T}=\hat{T}_{2,2}\circ\hat{T}_{1,2}=\hat{T}_{2,1}\circ\hat{T}_{1,1}.

We now extend Remark
2.8
(i) by showing that the relation
\leq
is transitive.

Theorem 2.9
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\mathcal{B}_{i}
be von Neumann algebras,
i=1,2,3
.
If
{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791ptH_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}\leq\,_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791ptK_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}
and
{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791ptK_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}\leq\,_{\mathcal{A}_{3}}\hskip-1.13791ptL_{\mathcal{B}_{3}}
, then
{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791ptH_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}\leq\,_{\mathcal{A}_{3}}\hskip-1.13791ptL_{\mathcal{B}_{3}}
.

Proof.

We need to complete the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791ptH_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791pt\widetilde{H}_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791pt\widetilde{\widetilde{H}}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791ptK_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791pt\widetilde{K}_{\mathcal{B}_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{A}_{3}}\hskip-1.13791pt\widetilde{\widetilde{K}}_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{A}_{3}}\hskip-1.13791ptL_{\mathcal{B}_{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{1,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{1,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{2,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{2,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{2,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{V_{2,2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{3,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{3,2}}

to a commuting diagram of the form

(7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791ptH_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791pt\widetilde{H}_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\hskip-1.13791pt\widetilde{L}_{\mathcal{B}_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791pt\widetilde{\widetilde{H}}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791ptK_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\hskip-1.13791pt\widetilde{K}_{\mathcal{B}_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{A}_{3}}\hskip-1.13791pt\widetilde{\widetilde{L}}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{A}_{3}}\hskip-1.13791pt\widetilde{\widetilde{K}}_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathcal{A}_{3}}\hskip-1.13791ptL_{\mathcal{B}_{3}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{1,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{1,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{2,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{1,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{2,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{3,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{2,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{2,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{V_{2,2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{3,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{3,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{3,2}}

for some quantum systems
{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\widetilde{L}_{\mathcal{B}_{3}}
and
{}_{\mathcal{A}_{3}}\widetilde{\widetilde{L}}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}
,
some
\mathcal{B}
-local isometries
W_{1,3}
and
W_{3,2}
, and
some
\mathcal{A}
-local isometries
W_{2,3}
and
W_{3,1}
.

Let
f_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
be a central projection, such that

\ker\pi_{K}|_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}=f_{2}^{\perp}\mathcal{A}_{2}
. We have that

\pi_{K}|_{f_{2}\mathcal{A}_{2}}:f_{2}\mathcal{A}_{2}\to\pi_{K}(\mathcal{A}_{2})
is a normal unital *-isomorphism;
in the rest of the proof, we denote by
\pi_{K}^{-1}
its inverse, from

\pi_{K}(\mathcal{A}_{2})
onto
f_{2}\mathcal{A}_{2}
.
By the
\mathcal{A}
-locality of
V_{2,2}
, there exists
a (unique) projection
e_{2}\in f_{2}\mathcal{A}_{2}
such that
p_{2}:=V_{2,2}V_{2,2}^{*}=\pi_{K}(e_{2})
.
Let
p_{\mathcal{A}}=\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(e_{2})\in\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{K})
,
and set
\widetilde{L}:=p_{\mathcal{A}}\widetilde{K}
.

Recalling that

\pi_{K}^{-1}(V_{2,2}\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(a_{1})V_{2,2}^{*})\in f_{2}\mathcal{A}_{2}
,
define
\pi^{\mathcal{A}}_{\widetilde{L}}:\mathcal{A}_{1}\to\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{L})
by

\pi^{\mathcal{A}}_{\widetilde{L}}(a_{1})=\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(\pi_{K}^{-1}(V_{2,2}\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(a_{1})V_{2,2}^{*})),\ \ \ a_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}.

Note that the
\mathcal{A}
-locality of
V_{2,2}
implies
V_{2,2}\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(a_{1})V_{2,2}^{*}\in\pi_{K}(\mathcal{A}_{2})
, so by
the fact that
V_{2,2}
is an isometry,

\pi^{\mathcal{A}}_{\widetilde{L}}
is a well-defined normal
*
-homomorphism. It is also unital as
\pi^{\mathcal{A}}_{\widetilde{L}}(1_{\mathcal{A}_{1}})=p_{\mathcal{A}}=1_{\mathcal{B}(p_{\mathcal{A}}\widetilde{K})}
.

Next, define
\pi^{\mathcal{B}}_{\widetilde{L}}:\mathcal{B}_{3}^{o}\to\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{L})
by
letting

\pi^{\mathcal{B}}_{\widetilde{L}}(b_{3}^{o})=p_{\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(b_{3}^{o})p_{\mathcal{A}},\ \ \ b^{o}\in\mathcal{B}_{3}^{o}.

Since
\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(\mathcal{A}_{2})\subseteq\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(\mathcal{B}_{3}^{o})^{\prime}
, we have that

p_{\mathcal{A}}\in\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(\mathcal{B}_{3}^{o})^{\prime}
, and hence the map

\pi^{\mathcal{B}}_{\widetilde{L}}
is a normal unital
*
-homomorphism. The inclusion

\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(\mathcal{A}_{2})\subseteq\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(\mathcal{B}_{3}^{o})^{\prime}
implies that
\pi^{\mathcal{A}}_{\widetilde{L}}
and
\pi^{\mathcal{B}}_{\widetilde{L}}
have commuting ranges, and therefore define a unital,
separately weak* continuous,
*
-homomorphism

\pi_{\widetilde{L}}:=\pi^{\mathcal{A}}_{\widetilde{L}}\times\pi^{\mathcal{B}}_{\widetilde{L}}:\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes_{\max}\mathcal{B}_{3}^{o}\to\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{L}),

turning
\widetilde{L}
into an
\mathcal{A}_{1}
-
\mathcal{B}_{3}
-bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\widetilde{L}_{\mathcal{B}_{3}}
.

Let
W_{1,3}=V_{2,3}V_{2,2}
.
Since

V_{2,3}V_{2,2}=V_{2,3}\pi_{K}(e_{2})V_{2,2}=\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(e_{2})V_{2,3}V_{2,2}=p_{\mathcal{A}}V_{2,3}V_{2,2},

we have that

W_{1,3}:\,_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}H_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}\to\,_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\widetilde{L}_{\mathcal{B}_{3}}
is a well-defined isometry.
Given
a_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
,
let
a_{2}
be the unique element of
f_{2}\mathcal{A}_{2}
such that

V_{2,2}\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(a_{1})V_{2,2}^{*}=\pi_{K}(a_{2})
. We have

\displaystyle W_{1,3}\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(a_{1})

\displaystyle=V_{2,3}V_{2,2}\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(a_{1})=V_{2,3}V_{2,2}\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(a_{1})V_{2,2}^{*}V_{2,2}

\displaystyle=V_{2,3}\pi_{K}(a_{2})V_{2,2}=\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(a_{2})V_{2,3}V_{2,2}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(\pi_{K}^{-1}(V_{2,2}\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(a_{1})V_{2,2}^{*}))V_{2,3}V_{2,2}=\pi_{\widetilde{L}}(a_{1})W_{1,3}.

Given
b_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}
, we have
V_{2,3}\pi_{K}(b_{2}^{o})V_{2,3}^{*}=\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(b_{3}^{o})
for some
b_{3}\in\mathcal{B}_{3}
. Let
e_{3}\in\mathcal{B}_{3}
be a projection for which
V_{2,3}V_{2,3}^{*}=\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(e_{3}^{o})
. Then

\displaystyle W_{1,3}\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(b_{2}^{o})W_{1,3}^{*}

\displaystyle=V_{2,3}V_{2,2}\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(b_{2}^{o})V_{2,2}^{*}V_{2,3}^{*}=V_{2,3}\pi_{K}(b_{2}^{o})V_{2,2}V_{2,2}^{*}V_{2,3}^{*}

\displaystyle=V_{2,3}\pi_{K}(b_{2}^{o})\pi_{K}(e_{2})V_{2,3}^{*}=V_{2,3}\pi_{K}(b_{2}^{o})V_{2,3}^{*}V_{2,3}\pi_{K}(e_{2})V_{2,3}^{*}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(b_{3}^{o})V_{2,3}\pi_{K}(e_{2})V_{2,3}^{*}=\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(b_{3}^{o})p_{\mathcal{A}}V_{2,3}V_{2,3}^{*}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(b_{3}^{o})p_{\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(e_{3}^{o})=\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(b_{3}^{o}e_{3}^{o})p_{\mathcal{A}}=\pi_{\widetilde{L}}(b_{3}^{o}e_{3}^{o}).

Hence,
W_{1,3}
is a
\mathcal{B}
-local map.
By Remark
2.8
, the composition
W_{1,3}V_{1,2}
is a

\mathcal{B}
-local map.

Let
W_{2,3}:\widetilde{L}\to\widetilde{K}
be the inclusion map. We have that

W_{2,3}
is
\mathcal{A}
-local.
In fact, condition (i’) from Definition
2.2
is tautological
because of the definition of
W_{2,3}
, while,
if
a_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
, there is unique
a_{2}\in f_{2}\mathcal{A}_{2}
such that

\displaystyle W_{2,3}\pi_{\widetilde{L}}(a_{1})W_{2,3}^{*}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(\pi_{K}^{-1}(V_{2,2}\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(a_{1})V_{2,2}^{*}))p_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(a_{2})\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(e_{2})=\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(a_{2}e_{2}),

implying
W_{2,3}\pi_{\widetilde{L}}(a_{1})W_{2,3}^{*}\in\pi_{\widetilde{K}}(\mathcal{A}_{2})
,
and thus condition (i”) from Definition
2.2
.

We similarly define a bipartite quantum system

\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}_{3}}\widetilde{\widetilde{{L}}}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}
, and isometries

W_{3,1}:\widetilde{\widetilde{{H}}}\to\widetilde{\widetilde{{L}}}
and

W_{3,2}:\widetilde{\widetilde{{L}}}\to\widetilde{\widetilde{{K}}}
. Using
symmetric arguments, we have that the operator

W_{3,1}
is
\mathcal{A}
-local, while the operator

W_{3,2}
is
\mathcal{B}
-local.
By Remark
2.8
,
the composition
V_{3,2}W_{3,2}
is
\mathcal{B}
-local, while
the compositions
W_{3,1}V_{1,1}
and
V_{3,3}W_{2,3}
are
\mathcal{A}
-local.

The relation
W_{2,3}W_{1,3}=V_{2,3}V_{2,2}
follows from the definition
of the operators
W_{1,3}
and
W_{2,3}
; similarly,

W_{3,2}W_{3,1}=\widetilde{V}_{2,2}V_{2,1}
.
The commutativity of the diagram (
7
)
is now immediate, and the proof is complete.
∎

3.
Models and self-tests

Our generalisation of self-testing is based on the
notion of an operator system;
we recall some basic facts and concepts, and refer the
reader to
[
45
]
for details.
If
H
is a Hilbert space and

\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(H)
is a linear subspace,
the space
M_{n}(\mathcal{S})
of all
n
by
n
matrices with entries in
\mathcal{S}

can be viewed as a subspace of
\mathcal{B}(H^{n})

after identifying
M_{n}(\mathcal{B}(H))
with
\mathcal{B}(H^{n})
.
If
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(H)
and
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(K)

(where
K
is a(nother) Hilbert space) are subspaces and

\phi:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{T}
is a linear map,
we let
\phi^{(n)}:M_{n}(\mathcal{S})\to M_{n}(\mathcal{T})
be the (linear) map, given by

\phi^{(n)}((a_{i,j})_{i,j})=(\phi(a_{i,j}))_{i,j}
.
An
operator system
is a subspace

\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(H)
,
such that
I_{\mathcal{H}}\in\mathcal{S}
and
s\in\mathcal{S}\Rightarrow s^{*}\in\mathcal{S}
.
Every operator system
\mathcal{S}
is an
abstract operator system
in the sense that
(a)
\mathcal{S}
is a linear *-space; (b) the real vector
space
M_{n}(\mathcal{S})_{h}
of all hermitian elements in the
*-space
M_{n}(\mathcal{S})

is equipped with a proper cone
M_{n}(\mathcal{S})^{+}
;
(c)

T^{*}M_{n}(\mathcal{S})^{+}T\subseteq M_{m}(\mathcal{S})^{+}
for all
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and all
T\in M_{n,m}
, and
(d) the cone family
(M_{n}(\mathcal{S})^{+})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

admits an Archimedean matrix order unit.
If
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{T}
are abstract operator systems,
a linear map
\phi:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{T}

is called
positive
if
\phi(\mathcal{S}^{+})\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{+}
,

completely positive
if
\phi^{(n)}
is positive for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

unital
if
\phi(I)=I
,
and a
complete order isomorphism
if
\phi
is completely positive, bijective, and
its inverse
\phi^{-1}
is completely positive.
We note that every unital completely positive map is
automatically contractive.
By virtue of the Choi-Effros Theorem
[
45
, Theorem 13.1]
,
every abstract operator system is completely order isomorphic to an
operator system.
A
state
of an operator system

\mathcal{S}
is a positive unital linear functional;
we denote by
S(\mathcal{S})
the (convex) set of all states of
\mathcal{S}
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be an operator system. Recall that
a pair
(C_{u}^{*}(\mathcal{S}),\iota)
is called a universal cover of
\mathcal{S}
, if
C_{u}^{*}(\mathcal{S})
is a unital
C^{*}
-algebra,
\iota:\mathcal{S}\to C_{u}^{*}(\mathcal{S})
is a unital complete order embedding
such that
\iota(\mathcal{S})
generates
C_{u}^{*}(\mathcal{S})

and, whenever
H
is a Hilbert space and
\phi:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{B}(H)
is a unital completely positive map, there exists a
*
-representation
\pi_{\phi}:C_{u}^{*}(\mathcal{S})\to\mathcal{B}(H)
such that
\pi_{\phi}\circ\iota=\phi

(see e.g.
[
31
]
). It is clear that
the universal cover is unique up to a canonical
*
-isomorphism.

We fix throughout this section operator systems
\mathcal{S}_{A}
and
\mathcal{S}_{B}
.
Their commuting tensor product
\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B}

is the operator system with underlying vector space the algebraic tensor product

\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{S}_{B}
, and matrix order structure inherited from the
inclusion
\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B}\subseteq C_{u}^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{A})\otimes_{\rm max}C_{u}^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{B})
; thus,
\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B}

sits completely order isomorphically in

C_{u}^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{A})\otimes_{\rm max}C_{u}^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{B})

(we note that we are using an equivalent definition of the
commuting tensor product to the original one,
see
[
31
, Theorem 6.4]
).
Given a Hilbert space
H
and
unital completely positive maps

\varphi_{A}:\mathcal{S}_{A}\to\mathcal{B}(H)
and
\varphi_{B}:\mathcal{S}_{B}\to\mathcal{B}(H)

with commuting ranges,
there exists a (unique) unital completely positive map

\varphi_{A}\cdot\varphi_{B}:\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B}\to\mathcal{B}(H)
,
such that

(\varphi_{A}\cdot\varphi_{B})(s\otimes t)=\varphi_{A}(s)\varphi_{B}(t),\ \ \ s\in\mathcal{S}_{A},t\in\mathcal{S}_{B}

(see
[
31
, Corollary 6.5]
).

A
quantum commuting model
over the pair
(\mathcal{S}_{A},\mathcal{S}_{B})

is a tuple

S=(_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{B}},\varphi_{A},\varphi_{B},\xi),

where
H
is a Hilbert space,
\xi\in H
is a unit vector,

\varphi_{A}:\mathcal{S}_{A}\to\mathcal{B}(H)
and
\varphi_{B}:\mathcal{S}_{B}\to\mathcal{B}(H)

are unital completely positive maps with commuting ranges,

\mathcal{A}
is von Neumann subalgebra of
\mathcal{B}(H)
,

\mathcal{B}
is a von Neumann algebra with
\mathcal{B}^{o}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(H)
,

H=\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}}H_{{\mathcal{B}}}
is a bipartite quantum system over

(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
, and the inclusions

\varphi_{A}(\mathcal{S}_{A})\subseteq\mathcal{A}
and

\varphi_{B}(\mathcal{S}_{B})\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{o}
hold.
We say that
S
is a
Haag model
if
\mathcal{B}^{o}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
We note that every pair
(\varphi_{A},\varphi_{B})
of
unital completely positive maps with commuting ranges, say,

\varphi_{A}:\mathcal{S}_{A}\to\mathcal{B}(H)
and
\varphi_{B}:\mathcal{S}_{B}\to\mathcal{B}(H)
,
and a choice of a unit vector

\xi\in H
, give rise to a canonical quantum commuting model
over the pair
(\mathcal{S}_{A},\mathcal{S}_{B})
by letting
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{B}^{o}
)
be the von Neumann algebra, generated by
\varphi_{A}(\mathcal{S}_{A})

(resp.
\varphi_{B}(\mathcal{S}_{B})
).

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{A}
and
\mathcal{S}_{B}
be operator systems, and
suppose that

S=(_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{B}},\varphi_{A},\varphi_{B},\xi)
and

\widetilde{S}=(_{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}\widetilde{H}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}},\widetilde{\varphi}_{A},\widetilde{\varphi}_{B},\widetilde{\xi})
are quantum commuting models over the pair
(\mathcal{S}_{A},\mathcal{S}_{B})
.

(i)

We say that
\widetilde{S}

is a
local dilation
of
S
, and write
S\preceq\widetilde{S}
,
if there exist a bipartite quantum system

{}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm aux}}(H_{\rm aux})_{{\mathcal{B}}_{\rm aux}}

over a pair of von Neumann algebras (
{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm aux}
,
{\mathcal{B}}_{\rm aux}
), a unit vector
\xi_{\rm aux}\in H_{\rm aux}
, and
a local isometry
V:\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{B}}\to\mbox{}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}\widetilde{H}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}\otimes\mbox{}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm aux}}(H_{\rm aux})_{{\mathcal{B}}_{\rm aux}}
such that

(8)

V\varphi_{A}(s)\varphi_{B}(t)\xi=(\tilde{\varphi}_{A}(s)\tilde{\varphi}_{B}(t)\tilde{\xi})\otimes\xi_{\rm aux},\ \ \ s\in\mathcal{S}_{A},t\in\mathcal{S}_{B}.

(ii)

We say that
\widetilde{S}

is an
approximate local dilation
of
S
, and write
S\preceq_{\rm a}\widetilde{S}
,
if there exist bipartite quantum systems

{}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{i,\rm aux}}(H_{i,\rm aux})_{{\mathcal{B}}_{i,\rm aux}}

over a pair of von Neumann algebras (
{\mathcal{A}}_{i,\rm aux}
,
{\mathcal{B}}_{i,\rm aux}
),
unit vectors
\xi_{i,\rm aux}\in H_{i,\rm aux}
, and
local isometries
V_{i}:\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{B}}\to\mbox{}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}\widetilde{H}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}\otimes\mbox{}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{i,\rm aux}}(H_{i,\rm aux})_{{\mathcal{B}}_{i,\rm aux}}
,

i\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

(9)

\left\|V_{i}\varphi_{A}(s)\varphi_{B}(t)\xi-(\tilde{\varphi}_{A}(s)\tilde{\varphi}_{B}(t)\tilde{\xi})\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}\right\|\to_{i\to\infty}0,\ \ \ s\in\mathcal{S}_{A},t\in\mathcal{S}_{B}.

We note that the reverse notation
\widetilde{S}\preceq S
was used in

[
44
]
to designate that
\widetilde{S}

is a local dilation of
S
, but we have decided to employ the
one specified in Definition
3.1
as it agrees
with the usual conventions in operator algebra theory; see e.g.

[
19
]
.

Remark 3.2
.

In self-testing examples, an ideal model
\widetilde{S}
is typically smaller (in the sense of Hilbert space dimension) than a given model
S
satisfying
S\preceq\widetilde{S}
(in line with the reverse notation from
[
44
]
). However, assistance from an auxiliary system together with a (typically entangled) auxiliary state is needed to witness the local dilation property
S\preceq\widetilde{S}
. We therefore view this property as a form of entanglement assisted local dilation.

Remark 3.3
.

By linearity, condition (
8
) is equivalent to

V(\varphi_{A}\cdot\varphi_{B})(u)\xi=(\tilde{\varphi}_{A}\cdot\tilde{\varphi}_{B})(u)\tilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{\rm aux}

being fulfilled for every
u
in the
completion of the tensor product
\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B}
.
In addition, choosing
u=1
, we have that

V\xi=\tilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{\rm aux}
.

An
\epsilon/3
-argument, together with the uniform boundedness of the
sequence
(V_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
appearing in (
9
), shows that
(
9
) is equivalent to the condition

(10)

\left\|V_{i}(\varphi_{A}\cdot\varphi_{B})(u)\xi-(\tilde{\varphi}_{A}\cdot\tilde{\varphi}_{B})(u)\tilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}\right\|\to_{i\to\infty}0,

for all
u
in the completion of the tensor product
\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B}
.
Choosing
u=1
, we have, in particular,

\left\|V_{i}\xi-\tilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}\right\|\to_{i\to\infty}0.

It is clear that

S\preceq\widetilde{S}\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ S\preceq_{\rm a}\widetilde{S}.

Proposition 3.4
.

The relations
\preceq
and
\preceq_{\rm a}
are preorders.

Proof.

Assume that
S\preceq_{\rm a}\widetilde{S}
and
\widetilde{S}\preceq_{\rm a}\widetilde{\widetilde{S}}
,
realised via sequences
(V_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\widetilde{V}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

of local isometries, where

V_{i}:\mbox{}_{{\mathcal{A}}}{H}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\to\mbox{}_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}}{\widetilde{H}}_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}}\otimes\mbox{}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}_{i,\rm aux}}({H}_{i,\rm aux})_{{{\mathcal{B}}}_{i,\rm aux}}

and

\widetilde{V}_{i}:\mbox{}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}\widetilde{H}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}\to\mbox{}_{\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}}\widetilde{\widetilde{H}}_{\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}}\otimes\mbox{}_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{i,\rm aux}}(\widetilde{H}_{i,\rm aux})_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}_{i,\rm aux}}
,

i\in\mathbb{N}
.
By Remark
2.8
,

\widetilde{V}_{i}\otimes I_{H_{i,\rm aux}}
is a local isometry from
{}_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}}{\widetilde{H}}_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}}\otimes\mbox{}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}_{i,\rm aux}}({H}_{\rm aux})_{{{\mathcal{B}}}_{i,\rm aux}}
to
{}_{\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}}\widetilde{\widetilde{H}}_{\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}}\otimes\mbox{}_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{i,\rm aux}}(\widetilde{H}_{i,\rm aux})_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}_{i,\rm aux}}\otimes\mbox{}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}_{i,\rm aux}}({H}_{i,\rm aux})_{{{\mathcal{B}}}_{i,\rm aux}}
.
It follows now from
Theorem
2.9
that
(\widetilde{V}_{i}\otimes I_{H_{i,\rm aux}})V_{i}
is a local isometry from
{}_{{\mathcal{A}}}{H}_{{\mathcal{B}}}
to
{}_{\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}}\widetilde{\widetilde{H}}_{\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}}\otimes\mbox{}_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{i,\rm aux}}(\widetilde{H}_{i,\rm aux})_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}_{i,\rm aux}}\otimes\mbox{}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}_{i,\rm aux}}({H}_{i,\rm aux})_{{{\mathcal{B}}}_{i,\rm aux}}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
Further, if
s\in\mathcal{S}_{A}
and
t\in\mathcal{S}_{B}
then

\displaystyle\left\|(\widetilde{V}_{i}\otimes I_{H_{i,\rm aux}})V_{i}\varphi_{A}(s)\varphi_{B}(t)\xi-(\widetilde{\widetilde{\varphi}}_{A}(s)\widetilde{\widetilde{\varphi}}_{B}(t)\widetilde{\widetilde{\xi}})\otimes\widetilde{\xi}_{i,\rm aux}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|(\widetilde{V}_{i}\otimes I_{H_{i,\rm aux}})V_{i}\varphi_{A}(s)\varphi_{B}(t)\xi-(\widetilde{V}_{i}\otimes I_{H_{i,\rm aux}})(\tilde{\varphi}_{A}(s)\tilde{\varphi}_{B}(t)\tilde{\xi})\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}\right\|

\displaystyle+\left\|(\widetilde{V}_{i}\otimes I_{H_{i,\rm aux}})\tilde{\varphi}_{A}(s)\tilde{\varphi}_{B}(t)\tilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}-(\widetilde{\widetilde{\varphi}}_{A}(s)\widetilde{\widetilde{\varphi}}_{B}(t)\widetilde{\widetilde{\xi}})\otimes\widetilde{\xi}_{i,\rm aux}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|V_{i}\varphi_{A}(s)\varphi_{B}(t)\xi-\tilde{\varphi}_{A}(s)\tilde{\varphi}_{B}(t)\tilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}\right\|

\displaystyle+\left\|(\widetilde{V}_{i}\otimes I_{H_{i,\rm aux}})\tilde{\varphi}_{A}(s)\tilde{\varphi}_{B}(t)\tilde{\xi}-(\widetilde{\widetilde{\varphi}}_{A}(s)\widetilde{\widetilde{\varphi}}_{B}(t)\widetilde{\widetilde{\xi}})\otimes\widetilde{\xi}_{i,\rm aux}\right\|.

It follows that

\left\|(\widetilde{V}_{i}\otimes I_{H_{i,\rm aux}})V_{i}\varphi_{A}(s)\varphi_{B}(t)\xi-(\widetilde{\widetilde{\varphi}}_{A}(s)\widetilde{\widetilde{\varphi}}_{B}(t)\widetilde{\widetilde{\xi}})\otimes\widetilde{\xi}_{i,\rm aux}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}\right\|\to_{i\to\infty}0.

Thus the family
((\widetilde{V}_{i}\otimes I_{H_{i,\rm aux}})V_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

implements an approximate dilation yielding the relation

S\preceq_{\rm a}\widetilde{\widetilde{S}}
, and showing that
the relation
\preceq_{\rm a}
is a preorder.
The fact that
\preceq
is a preorder now follows
after noticing that it is a special case of
\preceq_{\rm a}
,
implemented by constant sequences of local isometries.
∎

To each model
S=(_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{B}},\varphi_{A},\varphi_{B},\xi)
, we associate a linear functional

f_{S}:\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B}\to\mathbb{C}
by letting

f_{S}(u)=\langle(\varphi_{A}\cdot\varphi_{B})(u)\xi,\xi\rangle,\ \ \ u\in\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B},

and a linear functional

\tilde{f}_{S}:C^{*}_{u}(\mathcal{S}_{A})\otimes_{\max}C^{*}_{u}(\mathcal{S}_{B})\to\mathbb{C}
by letting

\tilde{f}_{S}(u)=\langle(\pi_{A}\cdot\pi_{B})(u)\xi,\xi\rangle,\ \ \ u\in C^{*}_{u}(\mathcal{S}_{A})\otimes_{\max}C^{*}_{u}(\mathcal{S}_{B}).

We note that
f_{S}
and
\tilde{f}_{S}

depend only on the tuple
(H,\varphi_{A},\varphi_{B},\xi)

and not on the von Neumann algebras
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
.
It is straightforward that if
S\preceq\widetilde{S}
then
f_{S}=f_{\widetilde{S}}
.
In fact, the same implication holds for approximate dilations.

Proposition 3.5
.

If
S\preceq_{\rm a}\widetilde{S}
then
f_{S}=f_{\widetilde{S}}
.

Proof.

Let

{}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{i,\rm aux}}(H_{i,\rm aux})_{{\mathcal{B}}_{i,\rm aux}}

be bipartite quantum systems,

\xi_{i,\rm aux}\in H_{i,\rm aux}
be unit vectors, and

V_{i}:\mbox{}_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{B}}\to\mbox{}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}\widetilde{H}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}\otimes\mbox{}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{i,\rm aux}}(H_{i,\rm aux})_{{\mathcal{B}}_{\rm aux}}

be local isometries,
i\in\mathbb{N}
, for which condition (
9
) holds.
Given
\epsilon>0
and
u\in\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B}
,
in view of (
10
), let
i\in\mathbb{N}
be such that

\left\|V_{i}(\varphi_{A}\cdot\varphi_{B})(u)\xi-(\tilde{\varphi}_{A}\cdot\tilde{\varphi}_{B})(u)\tilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}\right\|<\frac{\epsilon}{2}

and

\left\|V_{i}\xi-\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}\right\|<\frac{\epsilon}{2\|u\|}.

We have

\displaystyle\left|f_{\widetilde{S}}(u)-f_{S}(u)\right|=\left|\left\langle(\tilde{\varphi}_{A}\cdot\tilde{\varphi}_{B})(u)\tilde{\xi},\tilde{\xi}\right\rangle-\left\langle(\varphi_{A}\cdot\varphi_{B})(u)\xi,\xi\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle(\tilde{\varphi}_{A}\cdot\tilde{\varphi}_{B})(u)\tilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux},\tilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}\right\rangle-\left\langle V_{i}(\varphi_{A}\cdot\varphi_{B})(u)\xi,V_{i}\xi\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle(\tilde{\varphi}_{A}\cdot\tilde{\varphi}_{B})(u)\tilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux},\tilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}\right\rangle-\left\langle V_{i}(\varphi_{A}\cdot\varphi_{B})(u)\xi,\tilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle V_{i}(\varphi_{A}\cdot\varphi_{B})(u)\xi,\tilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}\right\rangle-\left\langle V_{i}(\varphi_{A}\cdot\varphi_{B})(u)\xi,V_{i}\xi\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon}{2}\left\|\tilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}\right\|+\frac{\epsilon}{2\|u\|}\left\|V_{i}(\varphi_{A}\cdot\varphi_{B})(u)\xi\right\|\leq\epsilon.

As
\epsilon
is an arbitrary positive real, we have that

f_{\widetilde{S}}(u)=f_{S}(u)
for every
u\in\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B}
.
∎

Definition 3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a class of quantum commuting models
over the pair
(\mathcal{S}_{A},\mathcal{S}_{B})
, and

\mathcal{S}
be a subset of states of the C*-algebra

C_{u}^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{A})\otimes_{\max}C_{u}^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{B})
.

(i)

We say that a state

f:\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B}\to\mathbb{C}
is a

self-test
for the class
\mathcal{C}
if there exists

\widetilde{S}\in\mathcal{C}
such that
f=f_{\widetilde{S}}
and,
whenever
S\in\mathcal{C}
is such that
f_{S}=f
, we have that

S\preceq\widetilde{S}
.
In this case, we say that
\widetilde{S}
is an
ideal model
for
f
.

(ii)

We say that a state

f:\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B}\to\mathbb{C}
is a

weak self-test
for the class
\mathcal{C}
if there exists

\widetilde{S}\in\mathcal{C}
such that
f=f_{\widetilde{S}}
and,
whenever
S\in\mathcal{C}
is such that
f_{S}=f
, we have that

S\preceq_{\rm a}\widetilde{S}
.
In this case, we say that
\widetilde{S}
is an
weak ideal model
for
f
.

(iii)

We say that a state
f:\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B}\to\mathbb{C}
is
an
abstract self-test
for
\mathcal{S}

if there exists a unique state
g\in\mathcal{S}
such that

g|_{\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B}}=f
.

It is clear that, in the notation of Definition
3.6
,
the state
f:\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B}\to\mathbb{C}
is a
self-test for the class
\mathcal{C}
if and only if
it is a self-test for the class

\mathcal{C}_{f}:=\{S\in\mathcal{C}:f_{S}=f\}
.

Remark 3.7
.

The setup of Definition
3.6
will be applied in
several different contexts in Section
5
.
The primary motivation behind it is the
self-testing paradigm for no-signalling correlations of quantum type
[
38
,
44
]
.
Letting
X,Y,A,B
be finite sets,

\mathcal{S}_{X,A}
(resp.
\mathcal{S}_{Y,B}
)
be the universal operator system of
|X|
(resp.
|Y|
)
POVM’s, each of cardinality
|A|
(resp.
|B|
, see (
28
)), Definition
3.6
(i) provides a commuting operator generalisation of the
usually employed notion of self-testing for quantum spacial systems (see e.g.
[
52
]
). We refer the reader to Subsection
5.2
for an extended discussion.

Note that
robust self-testing
for
no-signalling correlations arising from finite dimensional
models (see e.g.
[
58
, Definition 3.3]
) is a priori stronger than
the corresponding notion of
weak self-testing from Definition
3.6
(ii).

We recall that, if
H
is a Hilbert space,
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(H)

is a von Neumann algebra and
\omega:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{C}

is a normal positive functional, the
support
of
\omega

[
29
, Definition 7.2.4]

can be defined as
the smallest projection
p\in\mathcal{M}
such that
\omega(x)=\omega(pxp)
for all
x\in\mathcal{M}
.
For
\xi\in H
, write
\omega_{\xi}
for the vector state, given by

\omega_{\xi}(x)=\langle x\xi,\xi\rangle
,
x\in\mathcal{B}(H)
.
Note that, if
p
is the support of the functional
\omega_{\xi}|_{\mathcal{M}}
then

(11)

p\xi=\xi.

Indeed,

\|\xi-p\xi\|^{2}=\langle\xi,\xi\rangle-\langle\xi,p\xi\rangle-\langle p\xi,\xi\rangle+\langle p\xi,p\xi\rangle=\omega_{\xi}(1)-\omega_{\xi}(p)=0,

and (
11
) follows.

Given a quantum commuting model
S=(_{\mathcal{A}}\hskip-1.13791ptH_{\mathcal{B}^{o}},\varphi_{A},\varphi_{B},\xi)
,
where
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}^{o}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(H)
,
we let
\epsilon_{A}\in\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\epsilon_{B}\in\mathcal{B}^{o}
)
be the support projection of the state
\omega_{\xi}|_{\mathcal{A}}

(resp.
\omega_{\xi}|_{\mathcal{B}^{o}}
) of
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{B}^{o}
). Following
[
44
]
, we say that
S
is
centrally supported
if

\varphi_{A}(s)\epsilon_{A}=\epsilon_{A}\varphi_{A}(s)\ \mbox{ and }\ \varphi_{B}(t)\epsilon_{B}=\epsilon_{B}\varphi_{B}(t),\ \ s\in\mathcal{S}_{A},t\in\mathcal{S}_{B}.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(H)
be a von Neumann algebra and

\xi\in H
be a unit vector.
Assume that
\xi
is separating for
\mathcal{A}
.
Then, given
x\in\mathcal{A}
and
\epsilon>0
, there exists
y\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
such that
\|x\xi-y\xi\|<\epsilon
.

Proof.

Since
\xi
is separating for
\mathcal{A}
, it is cyclic for its commutant

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. The statement is now immediate.
∎

In what follows, for
\xi
,
\eta\in H
and
\epsilon>0
,
we write
\xi\sim^{\epsilon}\eta
if
\|\xi-\eta\|<\epsilon
.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(H)
be a von Neumann algebra and
\xi\in H

be a unit vector. Let
\epsilon_{A}\in\mathcal{A}
be the support
of the state
\omega_{\xi}|_{\mathcal{A}}
of
\mathcal{A}
,
and
p_{A}\in\mathcal{B}(H)
be the projection onto

\overline{\mathcal{A}\xi}
. Then
\xi\in\epsilon_{A}p_{A}H
, and

\xi
is cyclic and separating for the von Neumann subalgebra

\epsilon_{A}p_{A}\mathcal{A}\epsilon_{A}p_{A}
of
\mathcal{B}(\epsilon_{A}p_{A}H)
.

Proof.

Note that, since
p_{A}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
\epsilon_{A}\in\mathcal{A}
,
we have that
\epsilon_{A}p_{A}\mathcal{A}\epsilon_{A}p_{A}
is indeed
a von Neumann algebra acting on
\epsilon_{A}p_{A}H
.
By (
11
),
\epsilon_{A}p_{A}\xi=\xi
and hence

\omega_{\epsilon_{A}p_{A}\xi}(\epsilon_{A}p_{A}a\epsilon_{A}p_{A})=\omega_{\xi}(a)
,

a\in\mathcal{A}
.

Assume that
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
\epsilon_{A}p_{A}a\epsilon_{A}p_{A}\geq 0
and
\omega_{\xi}(p_{A}\epsilon_{A}a\epsilon_{A}p_{A})=0
. Then

0=p_{A}\epsilon_{A}a\epsilon_{A}p_{A}\xi=\epsilon_{A}a\epsilon_{A}p_{A}\xi=\epsilon_{A}a\epsilon_{A}\xi;

thus
\epsilon_{A}a^{*}\epsilon_{A}a\epsilon_{A}\xi=0
and hence

\omega_{\xi}(\epsilon_{A}a^{*}\epsilon_{A}a\epsilon_{A})=0
. As
\omega_{\xi}
is faithful
on
\epsilon_{A}\mathcal{A}\epsilon_{A}
, we get
\epsilon_{A}a^{*}\epsilon_{A}a\epsilon_{A}=0
and hence
\epsilon_{A}a\epsilon_{A}=0
.
This implies that
\omega_{\xi}
is faithful on
\epsilon_{A}p_{A}\mathcal{A}\epsilon_{A}p_{A}
and therefore,
\xi
is separating for
\epsilon_{A}p_{A}\mathcal{A}\epsilon_{A}p_{A}
.

Let
\eta\in H
be such that

\langle\epsilon_{A}p_{A}a\epsilon_{A}p_{A}\xi,\epsilon_{A}p_{A}\eta\rangle=0

for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
. Then

\langle a\xi,p_{A}\epsilon_{A}\eta\rangle=0
and,
since
p_{A}\epsilon_{A}\eta\in p_{A}H
, while

p_{A}H=\overline{\mathcal{A}\xi}
,
we have
p_{A}\epsilon_{A}\eta=0
, showing that
\xi
is cyclic.
∎

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space,
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(H)
be a von Neumann algebra,

\xi\in H
, and
\epsilon_{A}
be the support projection of
\omega_{\xi}|_{\mathcal{A}}
.
Suppose that for every selfadjoint
a\in\mathcal{A}
and
every
\epsilon>0
, there exists

\hat{a}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
such that
a\xi\sim^{\epsilon}\hat{a}\xi
. Then
[\epsilon_{A},a]=0

for every
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

By (
11
),
\epsilon_{A}\xi=\xi
.
Thus

\epsilon_{A}a\xi\sim^{\epsilon}\epsilon_{A}\hat{a}\xi=\hat{a}\epsilon_{A}\xi=\hat{a}\xi\sim^{\epsilon}a\xi.

Therefore
\omega_{\xi}(a(1-\epsilon_{A})a)=0
and, as

\epsilon_{A}
is the support projection of
\omega_{\xi}|_{\mathcal{A}}

and
a(1-\epsilon_{A})a
is positive, we obtain

\epsilon_{A}a(1-\epsilon_{A})a\epsilon_{A}=0
, and hence
(1-\epsilon_{A})a\epsilon_{A}=0
,
that is,

a\epsilon_{A}=\epsilon_{A}a\epsilon_{A}
. As
a
is selfadjoint,
this implies
\epsilon_{A}a=a\epsilon_{A}
.
∎

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
S=(_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{B}},\varphi_{A},\varphi_{B},\xi)
and

\widetilde{S}=(_{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}\widetilde{H}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}},\widetilde{\varphi}_{A},\widetilde{\varphi}_{B},\widetilde{\xi})
be quantum commuting models
over
(\mathcal{S}_{A},\mathcal{S}_{B})
.
Suppose that
S\preceq_{\rm a}\widetilde{S}
and that
\widetilde{S}
is centrally supported.
Then the states

\omega_{\xi}\circ\pi_{\varphi_{A}\cdot\varphi_{B}}

and
\omega_{\widetilde{\xi}}\circ\pi_{\widetilde{\varphi}_{A}\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}_{B}}
of
C^{*}_{u}(\mathcal{S}_{A})\otimes_{\max}C^{*}_{u}(\mathcal{S}_{B})
coincide.

Proof.

We write
\varphi=\varphi_{A}\cdot\varphi_{B}
and

\widetilde{\varphi}=\widetilde{\varphi}_{A}\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}_{B}
for brevity.
It suffices to show that
(\omega_{\xi}\circ\pi_{\varphi})(a\otimes b)=(\omega_{\widetilde{\xi}}\circ\pi_{\widetilde{\varphi}})(a\otimes b)
holds for all
elements
a\in C_{u}^{*}(S_{A})
(resp.
b\in C_{u}^{*}(S_{B})
) of the form

a=s_{1}\ldots s_{n}
(resp.
b=t_{1}\ldots t_{n}
), where
s_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}

(resp.
t_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{B}
),
i=1,\dots,n
.
Write
\pi_{A}(a)=\pi_{\varphi}(a\otimes 1)
,
\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(a)=\pi_{\widetilde{\varphi}}(a\otimes 1)
,

\pi_{B}(b)=\pi_{\varphi}(1\otimes b)
and
\widetilde{\pi}_{B}(b)=\pi_{\widetilde{\varphi}}(1\otimes b)
,
a\in C_{u}^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{A})
,
b\in C_{u}^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{B})
.

Let
(V_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of local isometries,
where
V_{i}:H\to\widetilde{H}\otimes H_{i,\rm aux}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
implementing the preorder assumption
S\preceq_{\rm a}\widetilde{S}

(see Definition
3.1
(ii)), and write

V_{i}=V_{i,2,2}\circ V_{i,1,2}=V_{i,2,1}\circ V_{i,1,1}
, where

V_{i,1,2}
,
V_{i,2,1}
are
\mathcal{B}
-local (and
V_{i,1,1}
,
V_{i,2,2}
are
\mathcal{A}
-local).

We have that

(12)

V_{i,1,2}\pi_{A}(a)=\sigma_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(a))V_{i,1,2}\ \mbox{ and }\ V_{i,2,2}\sigma_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(a))=\rho_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(a))V_{i,2,2},

for
a\in C_{u}^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{A})
and
for some normal *-representations
\sigma_{A,i}
and
\rho_{A,i}
of
\mathcal{A}
, the latter mapping into
\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})\bar{\otimes}\pi_{H_{i,\rm aux}}(\mathcal{A}_{i,\rm aux})
.
Let
\widetilde{\epsilon}_{A}
be the support projection of
the restriction of
\omega_{\widetilde{\xi}}
to
\pi_{\widetilde{H}}({\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}})

and
\widetilde{p}_{A}
be the projection onto
\overline{\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})\widetilde{\xi}}
.
Set
\widetilde{q}_{A}=\widetilde{\epsilon}_{A}\widetilde{p}_{A}
. As
\widetilde{S}
is centrally supported,

(13)

\widetilde{\epsilon}_{A}\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(a)=\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(a)\widetilde{\epsilon}_{A},\ \ \ a\in C_{u}^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{A}),

and hence

(14)

\widetilde{q}_{A}\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(a)=\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(a)\widetilde{q}_{A},\ \ \ a\in C_{u}^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{A}).

We claim that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, contractions
s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}
,
\epsilon>0
, there exists
i_{0}
such that,
setting
a=s_{1}\cdots s_{n}
, if
i\geq i_{0}
then

(15)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(a)\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}

\displaystyle\sim^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\epsilon}_{A}\otimes I_{H_{i,{\rm aux}}})\rho_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(a))(\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}).

Let
n=1
,
s\in\mathcal{S}_{A}
be contractive and
\epsilon>0
. By the relation
S\preceq_{\rm a}\widetilde{S}
and (
12
), there exists
i_{0}
such that, if
i\geq i_{0}
then

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(s)\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}

\displaystyle\sim^{\epsilon/2}

\displaystyle V_{i,2,2}V_{i,1,2}\pi_{A}(s)\xi=V_{i,2,2}\sigma_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(s))V_{i,1,2}\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(s))V_{i}\xi

\displaystyle\sim^{\epsilon/2}

\displaystyle\rho_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(s))(\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}).

Claim (
15
) now follows from (
11
) and (
13
).

Assume the claim holds for
n>1
. Consider
a=s_{1}\ldots s_{n}s_{n+1}
, where each
s_{i}
is a contraction in
\mathcal{S}_{A}
, and let
\epsilon>0
. By hypothesis (and the case
n=1
), there is an
i_{0}
such that for every
i\geq i_{0}
,

(16)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(a^{\prime})\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}

\displaystyle\sim^{\epsilon/5}

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\epsilon}_{A}\otimes 1_{H_{i,{\rm aux}}})\rho_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(a^{\prime}))(\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}),

where
a^{\prime}=s_{1}\ldots s_{n}
or
a^{\prime}=s_{n+1}
. By Lemmas
3.8
and
3.9
,
there exists

\tilde{a}\in(\widetilde{q}_{A}\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})\widetilde{q}_{A})^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{q}_{A}\widetilde{H})
such that

\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(s_{n+1})\widetilde{\xi}=\widetilde{q}_{A}\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(s_{n+1})\widetilde{q}_{A}\widetilde{\xi}\sim^{\epsilon/5}\tilde{a}\widetilde{\xi}.

Using (
12
), (
13
), (
14
),
(
16
) and
the fact that

\rho_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(a^{\prime}))\in\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})\bar{\otimes}\pi_{H_{i,\rm aux}}(\mathcal{A}_{i,\rm aux}),

for
i\geq i_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91684pt\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(a^{\prime})\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(s_{n+1})\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}\sim^{\epsilon/5}\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(a^{\prime})\tilde{a}\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{q}_{A}\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(a^{\prime})\widetilde{q}_{A}\tilde{a}\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}=(\tilde{a}\otimes 1_{i,\rm aux})(\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(a^{\prime})\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux})

\displaystyle\sim^{\epsilon/5}(\tilde{a}\otimes 1_{i,\rm aux})(\widetilde{\epsilon}_{A}\otimes 1_{H_{i,\rm aux}})\rho_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(a^{\prime}))(\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux})

\displaystyle=(\widetilde{q}_{A}\otimes 1_{H_{i,{\rm aux}}})\rho_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(a^{\prime}))(\tilde{a}\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}})

\displaystyle\sim^{\epsilon/5}(\widetilde{q}_{A}\otimes 1_{H_{i,\rm aux}})\rho_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(a^{\prime}))(\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(s_{n+1})\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}})

\displaystyle\sim^{\epsilon/5}(\widetilde{q}_{A}\otimes 1_{H_{i,\rm aux}})\rho_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(a^{\prime}))V_{i,2,2}V_{i,1,2}\pi_{A}(s_{n+1})\xi

\displaystyle=(\widetilde{q}_{A}\otimes 1_{H_{i,\rm aux}})\rho_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(a^{\prime}))(V_{i,2,2}\sigma_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(s_{n+1}))V_{i,1,2}\xi

\displaystyle=(\widetilde{q}_{A}\otimes 1_{H_{i,{\rm aux}}})\rho_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(a^{\prime}s_{n+1}))V_{i,2,2}V_{i,1,2}\xi

\displaystyle=(\widetilde{q}_{A}\otimes 1_{H_{i,\rm aux}})\rho_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(a))V_{i}\xi

\displaystyle\sim^{\epsilon/5}(\widetilde{\epsilon}_{A}\otimes 1_{H_{i,{\rm aux}}})\rho_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(a))(\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}).

Thus,

\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(a)\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}\sim^{\epsilon}(\widetilde{\epsilon}_{A}\otimes 1_{H_{i,{\rm aux}}})\rho_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(a))(\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}).

A similar induction argument shows that,
if
\widetilde{\epsilon}_{B}
is the support projection of the restriction of
the functional
\omega_{\widetilde{\xi}}
on
\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(\mathcal{B}^{o})
, then for all monomials
b\in C_{u}^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{B})
whose individual terms are contractions in
\mathcal{S}_{B}
, and
\epsilon>0
, there is an
i_{0}
such that for
i\geq i_{0}
,

(17)

\widetilde{\pi}_{B}(b)\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}\sim^{\epsilon}(\widetilde{\epsilon}_{B}\otimes 1_{H_{i,{\rm aux}}})\rho_{B,i}(\pi_{B}(b))(\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}),

where
\rho_{B,i}
is the normal
*
-representation of
\mathcal{B}^{o}
constructed from the locality of
V_{i}
, analogous to
\rho_{A,i}
.
We have that

\widetilde{\epsilon}_{A}\in\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})
,
\widetilde{\epsilon}_{B}\in\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{o})
and
[\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(x),\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(y^{o})]=0
,
x\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
,
y\in\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
.
Using (
11
), (
13
), (
15
), (
17
), the relations

[\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(a)\otimes 1_{H_{i,\rm aux}},\rho_{B,i}(\pi_{B}(b))]=0

and the fact that
V_{i,2,2}V_{i,1,2}=V_{i,2,1}V_{i,1,1}
,
for monomials
a\in C_{u}^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{A})
and
b\in C_{u}^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{B})
, whose individual terms are contractions in
\mathcal{S}_{A}
and
\mathcal{S}_{B}
, respectively and
\epsilon>0
, there is an
i_{0}
such that for
i\geq i_{0}
, we obtain

\displaystyle(\omega_{\widetilde{\xi}}\circ\pi_{\widetilde{\varphi}})(a\otimes b)=\langle\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(a)\widetilde{\pi}_{B}(b)\widetilde{\xi},\widetilde{\xi}\rangle=\langle\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(a)\widetilde{\pi}_{B}(b)\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}},\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}\rangle

\displaystyle\sim^{\epsilon/4}\langle(\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(a)\widetilde{\epsilon}_{B}\otimes 1_{H_{i,{\rm aux}}})\rho_{B,i}(\pi_{B}(b))(\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}),\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\rho_{B,i}(\pi_{B}(b))(\widetilde{\pi}_{A}(a)\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}),\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}\rangle

\displaystyle\sim^{\epsilon/4}\langle\rho_{B,i}(\pi_{B}(b))(\widetilde{\epsilon}_{A}\otimes 1_{H_{i,{\rm aux}}})\rho_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(a))(\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}),\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}\rangle

\displaystyle\sim^{\epsilon/4}\langle\rho_{B,i}(\pi_{B}(b))\rho_{A,i}(\pi_{A}(a))V_{i,2,2}V_{i,1,2}\xi,\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\rho_{B,i}(\pi_{B}(b))V_{i,2,2}V_{i,1,2}\pi_{A}(a)\xi,\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\rho_{B,i}(\pi_{B}(b))V_{i,2,1}V_{i,1,1}\pi_{A}(a)\xi,\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle V_{i,2,1}V_{i,1,1}\pi_{B}(b)\pi_{A}(a)\xi,\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\pi_{B}(b)\pi_{A}(a)\xi,V_{i}^{*}(\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}})\rangle

\displaystyle\sim^{\epsilon/4}\langle\pi_{B}(b)\pi_{A}(a)\xi,\xi\rangle=(\omega_{\xi}\circ\pi_{\varphi})(a\otimes b).

Since
\epsilon
is an arbitrary positive real, we conclude that

(\omega_{\widetilde{\xi}}\circ\pi_{\widetilde{\varphi}})(a\otimes b)=(\omega_{\xi}\circ\pi_{\varphi})(a\otimes b).

By linearity, density and continuity it follows that
\omega_{\xi}\circ\pi_{\varphi_{A}\cdot\varphi_{B}}=\omega_{\widetilde{\xi}}\circ\pi_{\widetilde{\varphi}_{A}\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}_{B}}
.
∎

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
S
and
\widetilde{S}
be models of a functional
f:\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B}\to\mathbb{C}

such that
S\preceq_{\rm a}\widetilde{S}
. If
S
is a centrally supported Haag model then
the model
\widetilde{S}
is centrally supported.

Proof.

Let
S=(_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{B}},\varphi_{A},\varphi_{B},\xi)
and

\widetilde{S}=(_{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}\widetilde{H}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}},\widetilde{\varphi}_{A},\widetilde{\varphi}_{B},\widetilde{\xi})
,
and suppose that

V_{i}=V_{i,2,2}V_{i,1,2}=V_{i,2,1}V_{i,1,1}

is a local isometry from
H
to

\widetilde{H}\otimes H_{i,\rm aux}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
, such that the
sequence
(V_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
implements the relation
S\preceq_{\rm a}\widetilde{S}
; here

V_{i,1,2}:H\to L_{i}
and
V_{i,2,2}:L_{i}\to\widetilde{H}\otimes H_{i,\rm aux}
, for some
Hilbert space
L_{i}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
\epsilon>0
and
s\in\mathcal{S}_{A}
be self-adjoint.
Since
S
is centrally supported,
\epsilon_{A}p_{A}\in\varphi_{A}(\mathcal{S}_{A})^{\prime}
,
and thus

\varphi_{A}(s)\xi=\epsilon_{A}p_{A}\varphi_{A}(s)\epsilon_{A}p_{A}\xi.

Noting the equality
(\epsilon_{A}p_{A}\pi_{H}(\mathcal{A})\epsilon_{A}p_{A})^{\prime}=\epsilon_{A}p_{A}\pi_{H}(\mathcal{A})^{\prime}\epsilon_{A}p_{A}
,
by Lemmas
3.8
and
3.9
,
there exists

x\in\pi_{H}(\mathcal{A})^{\prime}
,
x\neq 0
, such that

\varphi_{A}(s)\xi\sim^{\epsilon}\epsilon_{A}p_{A}x\epsilon_{A}p_{A}\xi=p_{A}x\xi.

Choose
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\left\|V_{i}\varphi_{A}(s)\xi-\tilde{\varphi}_{A}(s)\tilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}\right\|<\min\left\{\epsilon,\frac{\epsilon}{\|x\|}\right\}

and

\left\|V_{i}\xi-\tilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}\right\|<\frac{\epsilon}{\|x\|}.

We have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi}_{A}(s)\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}

\displaystyle\sim^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle V_{i,2,2}V_{i,1,2}\varphi_{A}(s)\xi\sim^{\epsilon}V_{i,2,2}V_{i,1,2}p_{A}x\xi

\displaystyle\sim^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle V_{i,2,2}V_{i,1,2}p_{A}xV_{i,1,2}^{*}V_{i,2,2}^{*}\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,\rm aux}.

By Haag duality,
p_{A}x\in\pi_{H}(\mathcal{A})^{\prime}=\pi_{H}(\mathcal{B}^{o})
; let
b^{o}\in\mathcal{B}^{o}
be such that

p_{A}x=\pi_{H}(b^{o})
, and note that

V_{i,2,2}V_{i,1,2}\pi_{H}(b^{o})V_{i,1,2}^{*}V_{i,2,2}^{*}\in(\pi_{H}(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{o})\bar{\otimes}\pi_{H_{i,{\rm aux}}}(\mathcal{B}_{i,{\rm aux}}^{o}))V_{i,2,2}V_{i,2,2}^{*}.

Let
y\in\pi_{\widetilde{H}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{o})\bar{\otimes}\pi_{H_{i,{\rm aux}}}(\mathcal{B}_{i,{\rm aux}}^{o})

be such that

V_{i,2,2}V_{i,1,2}\pi_{H}(b^{o})V_{i,1,2}^{*}V_{i,2,2}^{*}=yV_{i,2,2}V_{i,2,2}^{*}.

We have

\widetilde{\varphi}_{A}(s)\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}\sim^{3\epsilon}yV_{i,2,2}V_{i,2,2}^{*}(\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}})\sim^{2\epsilon}y(\widetilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}).

If
\epsilon_{i,{\rm aux}}^{A}
is the support projection of

\omega_{\xi_{i,{\rm aux}}}|_{\mathcal{A}_{i,{\rm aux}}}
, by Lemma
3.10
, we obtain

[\widetilde{\varphi}_{A}(s)\otimes 1_{H_{i,{\rm aux}}},\widetilde{\epsilon}_{A}\otimes\epsilon_{i,{\rm aux}}^{A}]=0
and hence

[\widetilde{\varphi}_{A}(s),\widetilde{\epsilon}_{A}]=0
. Similar arguments applied to
\widetilde{\varphi}_{B}
show that
\widetilde{S}
is centrally supported.
∎

Theorem 3.13
.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{A}
and
\mathcal{S}_{B}
be operator systems and

\mathfrak{M}
be a family of quantum commuting models over
(\mathcal{S}_{A},\mathcal{S}_{B})
.
Let
\mathcal{S}=\{\tilde{f}_{S}:S\in\mathfrak{M}\}
and

f:\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B}\to\mathbb{C}

is the restriction of an element of
\mathcal{S}
.
Assume that
\mathfrak{M}
