s(u)=\tilde{\tau}\left(q_{\rm C}((\Theta\circ\partial)(u))\right),\ \ \ u\in\mathcal{S}_{X,\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\otimes\mathcal{S}_{X,\mathbb{Z}_{2}}.

Let
\tilde{\phi}:\mathcal{A}_{X,\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\otimes_{\max}\mathcal{A}_{X,\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\to\mathbb{C}
be an extension of
\tilde{s}
to
an element of
S(\mathcal{A}_{X,\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\otimes_{\max}\mathcal{A}_{X,\mathbb{Z}_{2}})
. Then

\tilde{\phi}
annihilates
\mathcal{J}_{\rm C}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{X,\mathbb{Z}_{2}}+\mathcal{A}_{X,\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\otimes\mathcal{J}_{\rm C}
and,
by the projectivity of the maximal tensor product,
induces a state

\phi:\mathfrak{C}_{X}\otimes_{\max}\mathfrak{C}_{X}\to\mathbb{C}
.
By the nuclearity of
\mathfrak{C}_{X}
, we may consider
\phi
as
a state on
\mathfrak{C}_{X}\otimes_{\min}\mathfrak{C}_{X}
.
By (
45
),
\phi
extends
s
, and hence
p_{\tilde{s}}
is a
Clifford correlation.
The claim now follows from (ii).
∎

5.5.
Self-testing for probabilistic assignments

Recall
[
1
]
that a contextuality scenario is a hypergraph
\mathbb{G}

with vertex set
X
(whose elements are thought of as outcomes of an experiment)
and hyperedge set
E
(whose elements are thought of as subsets of jointly measurable outcomes) with the property that
every vertex is contained in at least one edge; we write
\mathbb{G}=(X,E)
.
In this section, we show how our general setup offers a broad
context for self-testing for contextuality scenarios, hosting, in
particular, the Main Theorem of
[
4
]
.

A
probabilistic assignment
of a contextuality scenario
\mathbb{G}=(X,E)

is a function
p:V\rightarrow[0,1]
such that

\sum_{x\in e}p(x)=1,\;\text{ for every }\;e\in E.

The set of all probabilistic assignments of
\mathbb{G}
will be denoted by
\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{G})
;
we have that
\Gg(\mathbb{G})
is a convex, possibly empty, subset of
\mathbb{R}^{V}
.
We stress that the term
probabilistic model
in the place of
probabilistic assignment was used in
[
1
]
, but we have chosen
to employ the latter term because of
clash of terminology with the established use of the
term
model
in self-testing
[
38
,
44
]
.
A contextuality scenario that admits a probabilistic assignment will be called
non-trivial
;
all contextuality scenarios herein will be assumed to be non-trivial.

A
positive operator representation

(POR)
of
a contextuality scenario
\G=(V,E)
on a
Hilbert space
H
is a collection
(A_{x})_{x\in V}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(H)
of
positive operators, such that

\sum_{x\in e}A_{x}=I_{H},\;\text{ for every }\;e\in E.

The POR
(A_{x})_{x\in V}
is called a

projective representation

(PR)
of
\G=(V,E)
if

A_{x}
is a projection,
x\in V
.

It was shown in
[
2
]
that, given a
non-trivial contextuality scenario
\G=(V,E)
,
there exists an operator system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{G}}
, generated by
positive elements
e_{x}
,
x\in X
, such that the unital completely positive maps

\phi:\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{G}}\to\mathcal{B}(H)
correspond to POR’s
(A_{x})_{x\in V}
of
\G
via the
assignment
\phi(e_{x})=A_{x}
,
x\in V
; in particular, the probabilistic models of
\G

correspond in a one-to-one fashion to the states of
\mathcal{S}_{\G}
.
In addition, in
[
2
]
, the universal C*-cover
C^{*}_{u}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{G}})

was explicitly identified.

Let
C^{*}(\mathbb{G})
be the universal unital C*-algebra, generated by
projections
p_{x}
,
x\in V
, subject to the relations

\sum_{x\in e}p_{x}=1
,
e\in E
, and let

\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{G}}={\rm span}\{p_{x}:x\in V\},

considered as an operator subsystem of
C^{*}(\mathbb{G})
.
By the definition of
C^{*}(\mathbb{G})
,
the *-representations
\pi:C^{*}(\mathbb{G})\to\mathcal{B}(H)
correspond to
projective representations of
\mathbb{G}
on the Hilbert space
H
,
via the assignment
\pi(p_{x})=P_{x}
,
x\in V
.
Further
[
2
]
, the unital completely positive maps

\phi:\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{G}}\to\mathcal{B}(H)
correspond, via the assignment
\phi(e_{x})=A_{x}
,
x\in V
, precisely to POR’s
(A_{x})_{x\in V}
of
\G
that are

dilatable
in that there exists a Hilbert space
K
, an isometry

V:H\to K
and a PR
(P_{x})_{x\in V}
of
\mathbb{G}
on
K
such that

A_{x}=V^{*}P_{x}V
,
x\in V
.

Let
\G=(V,E)
and
\Hbb=(W,F)
be contextuality scenarios.
The pairs
(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{G}},\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{H}})
and

(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{G}},\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{H}})

determine finitary frameworks for self-testing.
We point out that, due to existence of a canonical
unital completely positive map

\iota_{\mathbb{G}}:\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{G}}\to\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{G}}

(resp.
\iota_{\mathbb{G}}:\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{H}}\to\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{H}}
)
[
2
]
,
self-testing over the pair
(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{G}},\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{H}})
is more
general than over that over
(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{G}},\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{H}})
.

A commuting operator model

S=(H,(A_{x})_{x\in V},(B_{y})_{y\in W},\xi)
of
(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{G}},\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{H}})

gives rise, via (
23
), to a correlation
p_{S}
which
is a probabilistic assignment
for the
product
contextuality scenario

\G\times\Hbb=(V\times W,E\dot{\times}F)
,
where
E\dot{\times}F=\{e\times f:e\in E,f\in F\}
.
Further,
p=p_{S}
is
no-signalling
in that

\sum_{x\in e}p(x,y)=\sum_{x\in e^{\prime}}p(x,y),\;\;y\in W,\;e,e^{\prime}\in E,

and

\sum_{y\in f}p(x,y)=\sum_{y\in f^{\prime}}p(x,y),\;\;x\in V,\;f,f^{\prime}\in F

(see
[
1
]
).
We let
\Gg_{\rm ns}(\G,\Hbb)
be
the set of all no-signalling probabilistic assignments of
\GH

(and write
\Gg_{\rm ns}=\Gg_{\rm ns}(\G,\Hbb)
in case
no confusion arises).
We note that the finitary framework
(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{G}},\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{H}})

gives rise to self-testing that includes as a special case the
POVM self-testing (see Section
5.2
); indeed,
if
X
and
A
are finite sets,
the
Bell scenario

\mathbb{B}_{X,A}=(V,E)
, defined by letting

V=X\times A
and

E=\{\{x\}\times A:x\in X\}
has the property that

\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{B}_{X,A}}=\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{B}_{X,A}}=\mathcal{S}_{X,A}

[
2
]
.

We now assume that

\mathbb{H}_{\circ}=(W,F)
, where
|W|=1
and
F=\{W\}
; in this case,

\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{H}_{\circ}}=\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{H}_{\circ}}=\mathbb{C}
and, hence,

\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{H}_{\circ}}=\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{G}}
.
We will see that, even in
this case, self-testing over the pair
(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{G}},\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{H}_{\circ}})

may be non-trivial.
We note that the projective models for a state
s\in S(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{G}})
now have the simpler form

M=(H,(P_{x})_{x\in V},\xi)
, where
H
is a Hilbert space,
(P_{x})_{x\in V}
is a
PR of
\mathbb{G}
, and
\xi\in H
is a unit vector; the corresponding state

s_{M}\in S(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{G}})

has the form
s_{M}(p_{x})=\langle P_{x}\xi,\xi\rangle
,
x\in V
.

Let
n
be an odd number with
n\geq 3
and
\mathbb{G}_{n}=(V_{n},E_{n})
, where

V_{n}=\{x_{i},x_{i,i+1}:i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}\}\ \mbox{ and }\ E_{n}=\{\{x_{i},x_{i+1},x_{i,i+1}\}:i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}\}.

Let
\mathfrak{M}^{(1)}
be the set of all PR models

(A_{x})_{x\in V_{n}}
for which
{\rm rank}(A_{x_{i}})=1

for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
,

\mathfrak{M}^{(1)}_{\max}=\left\{M\in\mathfrak{M}^{(1)}:\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}s_{M}(p_{x_{i}})=\max_{M^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{M}^{(1)}}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}s_{M^{\prime}}(p_{x_{i}})\right\},

and

\mathcal{S}^{(1)}=\{s_{M}:M\in\mathfrak{M}^{(1)}_{\max}\}
.
The following result is mainly a rephrasing of

[
4
, Main Theorem]

in the setup of the present paper.

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
n
be an odd number with
n\geq 3
.
Then
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}
admits a self-test.

Proof.

Each model in
\mathfrak{M}^{(1)}
has the form

(H,(P_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}},(P_{i,i+1})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}},\xi)
, where
{\rm rank}(P_{i})=1
for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, and

P_{i}+P_{i+1}+P_{i,i+1}=I_{H},\ \ \ i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}.

In particular, we have that

(46)

P_{i}+P_{i+1}\leq I_{H},\ \ \ i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}.

Conversely, if we are given rank one projections
(P_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
on
H

for which the inequalities (
46
) are fulfilled then, setting

P_{i,i+1}:=I_{H}-P_{i}-P_{i+1}
, we obtain a model
in
\mathfrak{M}^{(1)}
.
The claim now follows from

[
4
, Main Theorem]
.
∎

5.6.
A self-test for full graph colourings

In this subsection we consider self-testing for
classical-to-quantum no-signalling (CQNS) correlations,
exhibiting an example for the classical-to-quantum game
of complete graph colouring.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
with
d\geq 2
, and

\mathcal{B}_{d^{2},d}:=\underbrace{M_{d}\ast_{1}\cdots\ast_{1}M_{d}}_{d^{2}\hskip 1.42271pt\mbox{\tiny times}},

where the free product is amalgamated over the units.
Let
\{\epsilon_{x,a,a^{\prime}}:a,a^{\prime}\in[d]\}
be the standard matrix units of
the
x
-th copy of
M_{d}
in
\mathcal{B}_{d^{2},d}
.
Further, let

(47)

\mathcal{R}_{d^{2},d}:={\rm span}\{\{\epsilon_{x,a,a^{\prime}}:x\in[d]^{2},a,a^{\prime}\in[d]\},

viewed as an operator subsystem of
\mathcal{B}_{d^{2},d}
,
thus obtaining a finitary context
(\mathcal{R}_{d^{2},d},\mathcal{R}_{d^{2},d})
.
Each state
s:\mathcal{R}_{d^{2},d}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{R}_{d^{2},d}\to\mathbb{C}

gives rise to a trace preserving completely positive
map
\Gamma_{s}:\mathcal{D}_{d}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{d}\to M_{d}\otimes M_{d}
,
defined by

\Gamma_{s}(\epsilon_{x,x}\otimes\epsilon_{y,y})=\sum_{a,a^{\prime}=1}^{d}\sum_{b,b^{\prime}=1}^{d}s(e_{x,a,a^{\prime}}\otimes e_{y,b^{\prime}b}))\epsilon_{a,a^{\prime}}\otimes\epsilon_{b,b^{\prime}},\ \ \ x,y\in[d^{2}],

which is a
classical-to-quantum no-signalling (CQNS) correlation

in that it
satisfies a natural version of the no-signalling conditions
(
25
) and (
26
)
and which is further of
quantum commuting type
, denoted
\mathcal{CQ}_{\rm qc}

(see
[
55
, Section 7]
for the precise definitions).

Let
\mathcal{K}_{d}
be the complete classical graph on
d
vertices, and

\mathcal{Q}_{d}
be the complete quantum graph on
d
vertices, that is,

\mathcal{Q}_{d}=\{\Omega_{d}\}^{\perp}
, as a
subspace of the Hilbert space
\mathbb{C}^{d}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d}
,
where
\Omega_{d}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\sum_{a=1}^{d}e_{a}\otimes e_{a}

is the maximally entangled unit vector in dimension
d
.
The
graph homomorphism game

\mathcal{K}_{d^{2}}\mapsto\mathcal{Q}_{d}

was defined in
[
55
]
. Its
game algebra
is the
universal C*-algebra
{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{K}_{d^{2}},\mathcal{Q}_{d})
,
generated by elements
e_{x,a,a^{\prime}}
,
x\in[d^{2}]
,
a,a^{\prime}\in[d]
,
satisfying the relations

(i)

e_{x,a,a^{\prime}}e_{x,b^{\prime},b}=\delta_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}e_{x,a,b}
,
\sum_{a=1}^{d}e_{x,a,a}=1
for all
x\in[d^{2}]
;

(ii)

\sum_{a,b=1}^{d}e_{x,a,b}e_{y,b,a}=0
if
x\neq y
.

We note that
{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{K}_{d^{2}},\mathcal{Q}_{d})
is a
quotient of
\mathcal{B}_{d^{2},d}
, realised via the map

\epsilon_{x,a,a^{\prime}}\mapsto e_{x,a,a^{\prime}}
.

Remark 5.14
.

Suppose that
H
is a finite dimensional Hilbert space and let

\pi:{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{K}_{d^{2}},\mathcal{Q}_{d})\to\mathcal{B}(H)
be a
unital *-representation.
As the subalgebra generated by
e_{x,a,a^{\prime}}
is isomorphic to
M_{d}
,
for each
x\in[d^{2}]
there exists a (finite dimensional)
Hilbert space
K_{x}
and unitaries

V_{x}:H\to\mathbb{C}^{d}\otimes K_{x}
such that

\pi(e_{x,a,a^{\prime}})=V_{x}^{*}(\epsilon_{a,a^{\prime}}\otimes I_{K_{x}})V_{x},\ \ \ x\in[d^{2}],a,a^{\prime}\in[d].

Since
\pi
is unital,
{\rm dim}(K_{x})
is constant across
x\in[d^{2}]
.
Applying a further unitary, we may assume that
there exists a Hilbert space
K
with
K_{x}=K
for all
x\in[d^{2}]
.

By relation (ii) (see the paragraph before the formulation
of Remark
5.14
),

\sum_{a,b=1}^{d}\pi(e_{x,a,b})\pi(e_{y,b,a})=0
whenever
x\neq y
.
It follows that

\sum_{a,b=1}^{d}(\epsilon_{a,b}\otimes 1)V_{x}V_{y}^{*}(\epsilon_{b,a}\otimes 1)=0,\ \ \ x,y\in[d^{2}],\ x\neq y,

and hence, taking partial trace
\Tr_{M_{d}}
along
M_{d}
, we obtain

(48)

({\rm tr}_{d}\otimes{\rm id}_{\mathcal{B}(K)})(V_{x}V_{y}^{*})=0,\ \ \ x,y\in[d^{2}],\ x\neq y.

Remark 5.15
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
is a finite dimensional C*-algebra
and
\pi:{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{K}_{d^{2}},\mathcal{Q}_{d})\to\mathcal{A}
is a
unital *-homomorphism. Then, up to a unital *-isomorphism,

\mathcal{A}=\oplus_{i=1}^{k}M_{dn_{i}}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}

and some
n_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
,
i\in[k]
.
Indeed, since
{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{K}_{d^{2}},\mathcal{Q}_{d})
is a quotient
of
\mathcal{B}_{d^{2},d}
, the *-homomorpshism
\pi
gives rise to a
unital *-homomorphism
\tilde{\pi}:\mathcal{B}_{d^{2},d}\to\mathcal{A}
,
and therefore to a unital *-homomorphism
\tilde{\pi}_{0}:M_{d}\to\mathcal{A}
.
Assume, without loss of generality, that

\mathcal{A}=\oplus_{i=1}^{k}M_{m_{i}}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}

and some
m_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
,
i\in[k]
.
The projection
{\rm proj}_{i}:\mathcal{A}\to M_{m_{i}}
is a unital
*-homomorphism and hence
{\rm proj}_{i}\circ\tilde{\pi}_{0}:M_{d}\to M_{m_{i}}
is a unital *-homomorphism.
It follows that
d|m_{i}
,
i\in[k]
.

Following
[
55
]
, a
perfect quantum commuting strategy

(resp.
perfect quantum strategy
)
for the
graph homomorphism game
\mathcal{K}_{d^{2}}\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}_{d}

is a CQNS correlation of quantum commuting (resp. quantum) type

\Gamma:\mathcal{D}_{d^{2}}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{d^{2}}\to M_{d}\otimes M_{d}
, such that

\Gamma(\epsilon_{x,x}\otimes\epsilon_{y,y})
is supported in

\mathcal{Q}_{d}
whenever
x\neq y
, and

\Gamma(\epsilon_{x,x}\otimes\epsilon_{x,x})=\Omega_{d}\Omega_{d}^{*}
for every

x\in[d^{2}]
.
By
[
9
,
55
]
, a quantum CQNS correlation

\Gamma:\mathcal{D}_{d^{2}}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{d^{2}}\to M_{d}\otimes M_{d}

is a perfect quantum commuting strategy for
\mathcal{K}_{d^{2}}\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}_{d}

if and only if there exist a
tracial C*-algebra
(\mathcal{A},\tau)

and a unital *-homomorphism

\pi:{\rm Hom}(K_{d^{2}},\mathcal{Q}_{d})\to\mathcal{A}
, such that

\Gamma(\epsilon_{x,x}\otimes\epsilon_{y,y})=\sum_{a,a^{\prime}=1}^{d}\sum_{b,b^{\prime}=1}^{d}\tau(\pi(e_{x,a,a^{\prime}}e_{y,b^{\prime}b}))\epsilon_{a,a^{\prime}}\otimes\epsilon_{b,b^{\prime}},\ \ \ x,y\in[d^{2}];

we write
\Gamma=\Gamma^{\pi,\tau}
.

Recall that a
unitary error basis
in
M_{d}

is a basis of
M_{d}
with respect to the trace inner product that consists of
unitaries, in other words, a collection
\{u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d^{2}}
of unitaries in
M_{d}
, such that

{\rm tr}_{d}(u_{i}u_{j})=\delta_{i,j}
,
i,j\in[d^{2}]
.
In the rest of this subsection, we restrict to the case
d=2
.
Recall the set

(49)

\mathcal{P}=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-i\\
i&0\end{array}\right)\right\}

of Pauli matrices in
M_{2}
;
it will be convenient to temporarily denote them by

U_{x}
,
x\in[4]
, in the order they appear in (
49
).
We note that, by
[
34
]
,
if
\mathcal{E}
is a unitary error basis in
M_{2}
then
there exist unitary matrices
R
,
T\in M_{2}
and constants
c_{V}
,
V\in\mathcal{P}
, such that

\mathcal{E}=\{c_{V}RVT:V\in\mathcal{P}\}
.

Let

\pi_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}:\mathcal{B}_{d^{2},d}\to M_{2}
be the
*-homomorphism, given by

\pi_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}(e_{x,a,a^{\prime}})=U_{x}^{*}\epsilon_{a,a^{\prime}}U_{x}
, and

\Gamma_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}:\mathcal{D}_{4}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{4}\to M_{2}\otimes M_{2}

be the CQNS correlation, given by

\Gamma_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}(\epsilon_{x,x}\otimes\epsilon_{y,y})=\sum_{a,a^{\prime}=1}^{2}\sum_{b,b^{\prime}=1}^{2}{\rm tr_{2}}(\pi_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}(e_{x,a,a^{\prime}})\pi_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}(e_{y,b^{\prime},b}))\epsilon_{a,a^{\prime}}\otimes\epsilon_{b,b^{\prime}},

where
x,y\in[4]
,
and note that
\Gamma_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}
is a perfect quantum strategy
for the game
\mathcal{K}_{4}\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}_{2}
.

Theorem 5.16
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},\tau)
be a tracial von Neumann algebra with a faithful trace
\tau
,
and
\pi:{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{K}_{4},\mathcal{Q}_{2})\to\mathcal{A}
be
a unital *-homomorphism, such that

\Gamma^{\pi,\tau}=\Gamma_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}
.
Then there exist a tracial von Neumann algebra
(\mathcal{N},\tau_{\mathcal{N}})
, a trace preserving
*
-isomorphism
\rho:\mathcal{A}\to M_{2}\otimes\mathcal{N}
and a unitary
V\in M_{2}\otimes\mathcal{N}
such that

(50)

V^{*}\rho(\pi(e_{x,a,a^{\prime}}))V=U_{x}^{*}\epsilon_{a,a^{\prime}}U_{x}\otimes 1_{\mathcal{N}},\ \ x\in[4],\ a,a^{\prime}\in[2].

Proof.

Fix
x\in[4]
. Then
\{\pi(e_{x,a,a^{\prime}})\}_{a,a^{\prime}}
is a system of matrix units in
\mathcal{A}
. Let
q=\pi(e_{x,1,1})
. Then for
m\in\mathcal{A}
we have that

m_{i,j}:=\pi(e_{x,1,i})m\pi(e_{x,j,1})
is in
q\mathcal{A}q
and the map

\rho:\mathcal{A}\to M_{2}\otimes q\mathcal{A}q,\ \ m\mapsto\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}\epsilon_{i,j}\otimes m_{i,j}

is a normal
*
-isomorphism. Let
\mathcal{N}=q\mathcal{A}q
and
\tau_{\mathcal{N}}
be the restriction of
\tau
to
q\mathcal{A}q
.
Then

\displaystyle(\Tr_{2}\otimes\tau_{\mathcal{N}})(\rho(m))=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\tau_{\mathcal{N}}(m_{i,i})=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\tau(m\pi(e_{x,i,1})\pi(e_{x,1,i}))=\tau(m).

Thus
\rho
is a trace preserving
*
-isomorphism and hence
\Gamma^{\pi,\tau}=\Gamma^{\rho\circ\pi,\Tr_{2}\otimes\tau_{\mathcal{N}}}
. For simplicity of notation identify now
\mathcal{A}
and
M_{2}\otimes\mathcal{N}
.
As
\{\pi(e_{x,a,a^{\prime}})\}_{a,a^{\prime}}
,
x\in[4]
, and
\{\epsilon_{a,a^{\prime}}\otimes 1_{\mathcal{N}}\}_{a,a^{\prime}}
are systems of matrix units in
M_{2}\otimes\mathcal{N}
, by
[
26
, Lemma 2.1]
, there exists a unitary
V_{x}\in M_{2}\otimes\mathcal{N}
,
x\in[4]
, such that

\pi(e_{x,a,a^{\prime}})=V_{x}^{*}(\epsilon_{a,a^{\prime}}\otimes 1_{\mathcal{N}})V_{x}.

A direct calculation shows that

\Tr_{2}(U_{1}^{*}\epsilon_{a,a^{\prime}}U_{1}U_{3}^{*}\epsilon_{b^{\prime},b}U_{3})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\text{if }a=b=a^{\prime}=b^{\prime},\\
-1&\text{if }a=b\neq a^{\prime}=b^{\prime},\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

By (
48
),

(51)

(\tr_{2}\otimes{\rm id}_{\mathcal{N}})(V_{x}V_{y}^{*})=0,\ \ \ x\in[4],a,a^{\prime}\in[2].

Since
\Gamma^{\pi,\tau}=\Gamma_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}
, we have that

(52)

(\Tr_{2}\otimes\tau_{\mathcal{N}})((\epsilon_{a,a}\otimes I_{n})V_{1}V_{3}^{*}(\epsilon_{a,a}\otimes I_{n})V_{3}V_{1}^{*})=1,\ \ a\in[2],

and

(\Tr_{2}\otimes\tau_{\mathcal{N}})((\epsilon_{a,a^{\prime}}\otimes I_{n})V_{1}V_{3}^{*}(\epsilon_{a^{\prime},a}\otimes I_{n})V_{3}V_{1}^{*})=-1,\ a,a^{\prime}\in[2],a\neq a^{\prime}.

Writing
V_{3}V_{1}^{*}
as a
2\times 2
-block matrix

V_{3}V_{1}^{*}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A_{3}&B_{3}\\
C_{3}&D_{3}\end{array}\right),

with
A_{3}
,
B_{3}
,
C_{3}
and
D_{3}
in
\mathcal{N}
,
by (
52
)
we have that

\tau_{\mathcal{N}}(A_{3}^{*}A_{3})=\tau_{\mathcal{N}}(D_{3}^{*}D_{3})=1.

As
V_{3}V_{1}^{*}
is unitary,

A_{3}^{*}A_{3}+C_{3}^{*}C_{3}=1
, giving

\tau_{\mathcal{N}}(C_{3}^{*}C_{3})=0
and hence, since
\tau_{\mathcal{N}}
is faithful,
C_{3}=0
. Similarly,
B_{3}=0
.
From (
51
) we get
A_{3}+D_{3}=0
, that is
V_{3}V_{1}^{*}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right)\otimes u
, where
u
is unitary in
\mathcal{N}
.
Next, writing
V_{x}V_{1}^{*}
,
x=2,4
, in a block form

V_{x}V_{1}^{*}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A_{x}&B_{x}\\
C_{x}&D_{x}\end{array}\right),

applying (
51
) to
V_{x}V_{1}^{*}
and
V_{x}V_{3}^{*}=V_{x}V_{1}^{*}(V_{3}V_{1}^{*})^{*}
, we get
A_{x}+D_{x}=0
and

A_{x}u^{*}-D_{x}u^{*}=0
and hence
A_{x}=D_{x}=0
for
x=2,4
.

Next we observe that

\Tr_{2}(U_{1}^{*}\epsilon_{a,a^{\prime}}U_{1}U_{2}^{*}\epsilon_{b^{\prime},b}U_{2})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1,&a=a^{\prime}\neq b=b^{\prime},\\
1,&a=b^{\prime}\neq a^{\prime}=b\\
0,&\text{otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

implying

\tau_{\mathcal{N}}(C_{2}^{*}B_{2})=\tau_{\mathcal{N}}(B_{2}^{*}C_{2})=1

and

\tau_{\mathcal{N}}(B_{2}^{*}B_{2})=\tau_{\mathcal{N}}(C_{2}^{*}C_{2})=1.

By Cauchy-Schwartz, we obtain that
C_{2}=\theta B_{2}
for a unimodular constant
\theta
;
as
\tau_{\mathcal{N}}(C_{2}^{*}B_{2})=e^{-i\varphi}\tau_{\mathcal{N}}(B_{2}^{*}B_{2})
, we have that
\theta=1
and

V_{2}V_{1}^{*}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)\otimes v

for a unitary
v\in\mathcal{N}
.
Condition (
51
) applied to
V_{2}V_{4}^{*}=V_{2}V_{1}^{*}(V_{4}V_{1}^{*})^{*}
gives
vB_{4}^{*}+vC_{4}^{*}=0
and hence
B_{4}=-C_{4}
. Therefore,

V_{4}V_{1}^{*}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-i\\
i&0\end{array}\right)\otimes w

for a unitary
w\in\mathcal{N}
.
With this at hand, we conclude that, if

V=V_{1}
, then

V\pi(e_{x,a,a^{\prime}})V^{*}=(V_{x}V_{1}^{*})^{*}(\epsilon_{a,a^{\prime}}\otimes 1_{\mathcal{N}})V_{x}V_{1}^{*}=U_{x}^{*}\epsilon_{a,a^{\prime}}U_{x}\otimes 1_{\mathcal{N}},

x\in[4]
,
a,a^{\prime}\in[2]
,
completing the proof.
∎

It follows from
[
55
, Lemma 9.2]
that there
exists a *-isomorphism
\partial:\mathcal{B}_{4,2}\to\mathcal{B}_{4,2}^{\rm op}
, such that

\partial(e_{x,a,a^{\prime}})=e_{x,a^{\prime},a}^{\rm op}
,

x\in[4]
,
a,a^{\prime}\in[2]
.
For

w=e_{x_{1},a_{1},a_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots e_{x_{k},a_{k},a_{k}^{\prime}}
, for some
x_{i}\in[4]
,
a_{i},a_{i}^{\prime}\in[2]
,

i=1,\dots,k
,
set

\bar{w}:=\partial^{-1}(w^{\rm op})=e_{x_{k},a_{k}^{\prime},a_{k}}\cdots e_{x_{1},a_{1}^{\prime},a_{1}}.

Recalling the definition (
47
),
let
\mathcal{S}
be the set of states of

C^{*}_{u}(\mathcal{R}_{4,2})\otimes_{\rm max}C^{*}_{u}(\mathcal{R}_{4,2})

that factor through
{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{K}_{4},\mathcal{Q}_{2})\otimes_{\rm max}{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{K}_{4},\mathcal{Q}_{2})
.
Let
\tilde{s}_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}\in\mathcal{S}
be the state given by

\tilde{s}_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}(u\otimes v)={\rm tr}_{2}(\pi_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}(q_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}(q_{u}(u)\overline{q_{u}(v)}))),\ \ \ u,v\in C^{*}_{u}(\mathcal{R}_{4,2}),

where
q_{u}:C^{*}_{u}(\mathcal{R}_{4,2})\to\mathcal{B}_{4,2}
and

q_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}:\mathcal{B}_{4,2}\to{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{K}_{4},\mathcal{Q}_{2})

are the quotient maps,
and let
s_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}
be the restriction of

\tilde{s}_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}
to
\mathcal{R}_{4,2}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{R}_{4,2}
.

Corollary 5.17
.

The state
s_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}
is an abstract self-test for
the family
\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
s_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}
has a
unique extension to a state

\phi:\mathcal{B}_{4,2}\otimes_{\max}\mathcal{B}_{4,2}\to\mathbb{C}
. Fix such an extension
\phi
and note
that the canonical correlation
\Gamma_{\phi}:\mathcal{D}_{4}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{4}\to M_{2}\otimes M_{2}
associated with
\phi

coincides with
\Gamma_{s_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}}
.
By
[
9
, Theorem 3.2]
, there exists a
tracial von Neumann
algebra
(\mathcal{A},\tau)
and a *-representation

\pi:\mathcal{B}_{4,2}\to\mathcal{A}
,
such that

\phi(u\otimes v)=\tau(\pi(u\bar{v})),\ \ \ u,v\in\mathcal{B}_{4,2}.

Further,
\pi
canonically descends to a *-representation

\tilde{\pi}:{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{K}_{4},\mathcal{Q}_{2})\to\mathcal{A}
,
such that, if

\tilde{\phi}:{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{K}_{4},\mathcal{Q}_{2})\otimes_{\max}{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{K}_{4},\mathcal{Q}_{2})\to\mathbb{C}
is the
canonical functional, arising from
\phi
in view of the definition of the class
\mathcal{S}
, we have that

\tilde{\phi}(u\otimes v)=\tau(\tilde{\pi}(u\bar{v})),\ \ \ u,v\in{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{K}_{4},\mathcal{Q}_{2}).

Let
\rho:\mathcal{A}\to M_{2}\otimes\mathcal{N}

be the *-homomorphism, arising from
Theorem
5.16
(here
(\mathcal{N},\tau_{\mathcal{N}})

is a tracial von Neumann algebra).
Then, for
w\in{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{K}_{4},\mathcal{Q}_{2})
,
we have that

\tilde{\phi}(w)=\tau(\tilde{\pi}(w))=(\tau_{2}\otimes\tau_{\mathcal{N}})(\pi_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}(w)\otimes 1_{\mathcal{N}})=\tau_{2}(\pi_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}(w)).

The proof is complete.
∎

Let
\mathcal{C}
be the class of quantum models

(H_{A}\otimes H_{B},\varphi_{A},\varphi_{B},\xi)
of CQNS correlations, where

H_{A}
and
H_{B}
are finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces,

H_{A}\otimes H_{B}=\mbox{}_{\mathcal{B}(H_{A})}H_{\mathcal{B}(H_{B})^{o}}
is a bipartite
system defined by the canonical bimodule structure of
H_{A}\otimes H_{B}
,
and
\varphi_{A}
and
\varphi_{B}
extend to representations
\pi_{A}
and
\pi_{B}
of
\mathcal{A}:=\mathcal{B}_{4,2}
and
\mathcal{B}:=\mathcal{B}_{4,2}
, respectively.

Proposition 5.18
.

The state
s_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}
is a selftest for
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

We will apply Theorem
4.3
, see also
[
44
, Theorem 4.12]
. First we observe that
s_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}
is an extreme point in
the dual
(\mathcal{R}_{4,2}\otimes_{\rm min}\mathcal{R}_{4,2})^{\rm d}

of
\mathcal{R}_{4,2}\otimes_{\rm min}\mathcal{R}_{4,2}
. In fact, if
s_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}s_{i}
, where
\alpha_{i}\geq 0
,
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i}=1
, then the corresponding correlations
\Gamma_{s_{i}}
are perfect strategies for quantum colouring of
\mathcal{K}_{4}
.
Indeed, note that, if
J=\Omega_{2}\Omega_{2}^{*}
is the maximally entangled state in
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
, then

0=\Tr(\Gamma_{s_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}}(\epsilon_{x,x}\otimes\epsilon_{x,x})J^{\perp})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\Tr(\Gamma_{s_{i}}(\epsilon_{x,x}\otimes\epsilon_{x,x})J^{\perp})

and hence
\Tr(\Gamma_{s_{i}}(\epsilon_{x,x}\otimes\epsilon_{x,x})J^{\perp})
,
being non-negative, must be zero. Similar arguments show that

\tr(\Gamma_{s_{i}}(\epsilon_{x,x}\otimes\epsilon_{y,y})J)=0
for all
i
and all
x\neq y
.

Therefore
s(e_{x,a,a^{\prime}}\otimes e_{y,b,b^{\prime}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\tau_{i}(\pi_{i}(e_{x,a,a^{\prime}})\pi_{i}(e_{y,b^{\prime},b}))
, where
\pi_{i}:{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{K}_{4},\mathcal{Q}_{2})\to\mathcal{A}_{i}
are

*
-homomorphisms into finite dimensional algebras
\mathcal{A}_{i}
with trace
\tau_{i}
.
By Proposition
5.16
,

\tau_{i}(\pi_{i}(e_{x,a,a^{\prime}})\pi_{i}(e_{y,b^{\prime},b}))={\rm tr}_{2}(U_{x}^{*}\epsilon_{a,a^{\prime}}U_{x}U_{y}^{*}\epsilon_{b^{\prime},b}U_{y})

for all
i
and hence
s
is extreme.
The state
s_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}
has also a unique extension to
\mathcal{B}_{4,2}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{4,2}
and hence by Theorem
4.3
it is a self-test.
∎

5.7.
Schur products

Let
G
be a finite group, let
\pi:G\to\mathcal{U}(H_{\pi})
and
\rho:G\to\mathcal{U}(H_{\rho})
be irreducible representations of
G
, and let
\psi\in H_{\pi}\otimes H_{\rho}
be a unit vector. Then

u(s,t):=\langle(\pi(s)\otimes\rho(t))\psi,\psi\rangle,\ \ \ s,t\in G,

is a normalised positive definite function on
G\times G
and the associated

Schur multiplier

\Theta(u):\mathcal{B}(\ell^{2}(G))\otimes\mathcal{B}(\ell^{2}(G))\to\mathcal{B}(\ell^{2}(G))\otimes\mathcal{B}(\ell^{2}(G))
is the unital quantum channel satisfying

(53)

\Theta(u)(e_{s,s^{\prime}}\otimes e_{t,t^{\prime}})=u(s^{-1}s^{\prime},t^{-1}t^{\prime})e_{s,s^{\prime}}\otimes e_{t,t^{\prime}};

if further clarity is needed, we write
\Theta(u)=\Theta_{\pi,\rho,\psi}
.
Letting
\widetilde{E}_{s,s^{\prime},g,g^{\prime}}=\delta_{s,g}\delta_{s^{\prime},g^{\prime}}\pi(s^{-1}s^{\prime})
and
\widetilde{F}_{t,t^{\prime},h,h^{\prime}}=\delta_{t,h}\delta_{t^{\prime},h^{\prime}}\rho(t^{-1}t^{\prime})
,
it is straightforward to verify that

\widetilde{E}:=(\widetilde{E}_{s,s^{\prime},g,g^{\prime}})_{s,s^{\prime},g,g^{\prime}}
and

\widetilde{F}:=(\widetilde{F}_{t,t^{\prime},h,h^{\prime}})_{t,t^{\prime},h,h^{\prime}}
are unitaries and hence
the quadruple

S_{\pi,\rho,\psi}=(H_{\pi}\otimes H_{\rho},\widetilde{E},\widetilde{F},\psi)
is a
\mathcal{Q}_{\rm q}
-model for
\Theta(u)
.

A
unistochastic operator matrix (USOM)

is a stochastic operator matrix
E=(E_{x,x^{\prime},a,a^{\prime}})\in M_{X}\otimes M_{X}\otimes\mathcal{B}(H)
for which there exists a unitary
U:H^{X}\to H^{X}
such that
E_{x,x^{\prime},a,a^{\prime}}=U_{a,x}^{*}U_{a^{\prime},x^{\prime}}
.
A
\mathcal{Q}_{\rm q}
-model will be called
unitary
if it is defined via USOM’s
(E_{s,s^{\prime},g,g^{\prime}})_{s,s^{\prime},g,g^{\prime}}
and
(F_{t,t^{\prime},h,h^{\prime}})_{s,s^{\prime},g,g^{\prime}}
(see Section
6.3
). In particular, we have that
S:=S_{\pi,\rho,\psi}
is a unitary model.
If
H
and
K
are Hilbert spaces, we say that a vector
\psi\in H\otimes K
is

marginally cyclic
if

\overline{(\mathcal{B}(H)\otimes 1)\psi}=\mathcal{B}(H\otimes K)=\overline{(1\otimes\mathcal{B}(K))\psi}.

Let

\mathfrak{M}(u)=\{S_{\pi^{\prime}\rho^{\prime},\psi^{\prime}}:\mbox{ full rank unitary model s.t. }\Theta_{\pi^{\prime}\rho^{\prime},\psi^{\prime}}=\Theta(u)\}.

Theorem 5.19
.

If
\psi
is marginally cyclic in
H_{\pi}\otimes H_{\rho}
and

(54)

\mathrm{span}\{(\pi(s)\otimes\rho(t))\psi\psi^{*}(\pi(s^{-1})\otimes\rho(t^{-1})):s,t\in G\}=\mathcal{B}(H_{\pi}\otimes H_{\rho})

then
(H_{\pi}\otimes H_{\rho},\widetilde{E},\widetilde{F},\psi)
is a self-test for
\mathfrak{M}(u)
.

Proof.

Suppose that

(H_{A}\otimes H_{B},(E_{s,s^{\prime},g,g^{\prime}})_{s,s^{\prime},g,g^{\prime}},(F_{t,t^{\prime},h,h^{\prime}})_{t,t^{\prime},h,h^{\prime}},\xi)
is a full rank unitary model for
\Theta(u)
in
\mathcal{Q}_{\rm q}
. This means that
E_{s,s^{\prime},g,g^{\prime}}=U_{g,s}^{*}U_{g^{\prime},s^{\prime}}
and
F_{t,t^{\prime},h,h^{\prime}}=V_{h,t}^{*}V_{h^{\prime},t^{\prime}}
for some block operator unitaries
(U_{g,s})_{g,s}:H_{A}^{G}\to H_{A}^{G}
and

(V_{h,t})_{h,s}:H_{B}^{G}\to H_{B}^{G}
, the reduced densities

(\id\otimes\tr)(\xi\xi^{*})
and
(\tr\otimes\id)(\xi\xi^{*})
have full rank, and

\langle(E_{s,s^{\prime},g,g^{\prime}}\otimes F_{t,t^{\prime},h,h^{\prime}})\xi,\xi\rangle=\delta_{s,g}\delta_{s^{\prime},g^{\prime}}\delta_{t,h}\delta_{t^{\prime},h^{\prime}}\langle\pi(s^{-1}s^{\prime})\otimes\rho(t^{-1}t^{\prime})\psi,\psi\rangle.

In particular,

(55)

\langle(U_{s^{\prime},s^{\prime}}\otimes V_{t^{\prime},t^{\prime}})\xi,(U_{s,s}\otimes V_{t,t})\xi\rangle=\langle(\pi(s^{\prime})\otimes\rho(t^{\prime}))\psi,(\pi(s)\otimes\rho(t))\psi\rangle

for all
s,s^{\prime},t,t^{\prime}\in G
.
We now observe that the unitaries
(U_{g,s})_{g,s}
and

(V_{h,t})_{h,t}
are necessarily diagonal.
Since (under the trace-duality convention)

\Theta(u)(X^{\rm t})^{\rm t}=(\id\otimes\tr_{2,4})((U\otimes V)(X\otimes\xi\xi^{*})(U^{*}\otimes V^{*})),

setting
\rho_{\xi}=(\id\otimes\tr)(\xi\xi^{*})
, we have

\Theta(u)(\rho^{\rm t}\otimes 1)^{\rm t}=(\id\otimes\tr)U(\rho\otimes\rho_{\xi})U^{*}.

Since the transformation

\rho\mapsto\Theta(u)(\rho^{\rm t}\otimes 1)^{\rm t}

is a
\mathcal{D}_{G}
-bimodule map, if

\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m_{A}}
is an orthonormal basis for
H_{A}
,
it follows that the Kraus operator

(\id\otimes e_{i}^{*})U((\cdot)\otimes\sqrt{\rho_{\xi}}e_{j}):\ell^{2}(G)\to\ell^{2}(G)

belongs to
\mathcal{D}_{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{D}_{G}
for each
i,j\in[m_{A}]
. Hence,

U(1\otimes\sqrt{\rho_{\xi}})(x\otimes 1)=(x\otimes 1)U(1\otimes\sqrt{\rho_{\xi}}),\ \ \ x\in\mathcal{D}_{G}.

Since
\rho_{\xi}
, and hence
\sqrt{\rho_{\xi}}
, has full rank, it follows that
U(x\otimes 1)=(x\otimes 1)U
, so that
U\in\mathcal{D}_{G}\otimes\mathcal{B}(H_{A})
. Hence,
U_{s,s^{\prime}}=\delta_{s,s^{\prime}}U_{s,s}
with
U_{s}:=U_{s,s}\in\mathcal{U}(H_{A})
.
Without loss of generality, we may also assume
U_{e}=1
(indeed, if not,
redefine
U_{s}
as
U_{e}^{*}U_{s}
, noting that
E_{s,s^{\prime},g,g^{\prime}}=U_{g,s}^{*}U_{e}U_{e}^{*}U_{g^{\prime},s^{\prime}}
).
An analogous argument shows that
V_{t,t^{\prime}}=\delta_{t,t^{\prime}}V_{t,t}
with
V_{t}:=V_{t,t}\in\mathcal{U}(H_{B})
and we may assume that
V_{e}=1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{B}
)
be the (unital)
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{B}(H_{A})
(resp.
\mathcal{B}(H_{B})
)
generated by
\{U_{s}\}_{s\in G}
(resp.
\{V_{t}\}_{t\in G}
).
By the finite-dimensionality of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, there exist unitaries
W_{A}:H_{A}\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n_{A}}H_{A}^{i}\otimes K_{A}^{i}
and
W_{B}:H_{B}\to\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n_{B}}H_{B}^{j}\otimes K_{B}^{j}
such that

W_{A}aW_{A}^{*}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n_{A}}\sigma_{A}^{i}(a)\otimes I_{K_{A}^{i}},\ \ \ a\in\mathcal{A},

and

W_{B}bW_{B}^{*}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n_{B}}\sigma_{B}^{j}(a)\otimes I_{K_{B}^{j}},\ \ \ b\in\mathcal{B},

with
\sigma_{A}^{i}
and
\sigma_{B}^{j}
irreducible representations of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, respectively. Fix orthonormal bases
\{e^{i}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{d_{A}^{i}}
and
\{e^{j}_{l}\}_{l=1}^{d_{B}^{j}}
of
K_{A}^{i}
and
K_{B}^{j}
, respectively. It follows from (
55
) that

\displaystyle\langle(U_{s^{\prime}}\otimes V_{t^{\prime}})\xi,(U_{s}\otimes V_{t})\xi\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{A}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{B}}\sum_{k=1}^{d_{A}^{i}}\sum_{l=1}^{d_{B}^{j}}p_{i,j,k,l}\langle(\sigma_{A}^{i}(U_{s^{\prime}})\hskip-2.84544pt\otimes\hskip-2.84544pt\sigma_{B}^{j}(V_{t^{\prime}}))\xi_{i,j,k,l},(\sigma_{A}^{i}(U_{s})\hskip-2.84544pt\otimes\hskip-2.84544pt\sigma_{B}^{j}(V_{t}))\xi_{i,j,k,l}\rangle,

where
\xi_{i,j,k,l}\in H_{A}^{i}\otimes H_{B}^{j}
is the normalisation of
(\id\otimes e_{k}^{i}\otimes\id\otimes e_{l}^{j})^{*}\xi
, and

p_{i,j,k,l}=\lVert(\id\otimes e_{k}^{i}\otimes\id\otimes e_{l}^{j})^{*}\xi\rVert^{2}\geq 0,

and note that
\sum_{i,j,k,l}p_{i,j,k,l}=1
. Combining (
53
) and (
55
), we obtain a convex decomposition of the channel
\Theta(u)
, which is extreme within the set of completely positive trace preserving maps by (
54
) (see e.g.
[
35
, Theorem 3]
). Indeed,
setting
\psi_{(s,t)}:=(\pi(s)\otimes\varphi(t))\psi
,
s,t\in G
,
in the notation and terminology of
[
35
]
,
\{\psi_{(s,t)}\}_{(s,t)\in G\times G}
is a full set of vectors by (
54
) and
C_{(s,t),(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})}:=u(s^{-1}s^{\prime},t^{-1}t^{\prime})=\langle\psi_{(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})},\psi_{(s,t)}\rangle
.
Thus, for every
\lambda=(i,j,k,l)\in\Lambda=\{(i,j,k,l):p_{i,j,k,l}>0\}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle(\pi(s^{\prime})\otimes\rho(t^{\prime}))\psi,(\pi(s)\otimes\rho(t))\psi\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\langle(\sigma_{A}^{i}(U_{s^{\prime}})\otimes\sigma_{B}^{j}(V_{t^{\prime}}))\xi_{i,j,k,l},(\sigma_{A}^{i}(U_{s})\otimes\sigma_{B}^{j}(V_{t}))\xi_{i,j,k,l}\rangle.

It follows that

W_{\lambda}:H_{\pi}\otimes H_{\rho}\ni(\pi(s)\otimes\rho(t))\psi\to(\sigma_{A}^{i}(U_{s})\otimes\sigma_{B}^{j}(V_{t}))\xi_{i,j,k,l}\in H_{A}^{i}\otimes H_{B}^{j}

is a well-defined isometry for each
\lambda\in\Lambda
. Recalling that
\psi
is marginally cyclic
and that
\pi
and
\rho
are irreducible (so
\mathrm{span}\{\pi(s):s\in G\}=\mathcal{B}(H_{\pi})
and
\mathrm{span}\{\rho(t):t\in G\}=\mathcal{B}(H_{\rho})
) for any
\eta\in H_{\pi}\otimes H_{\rho}
and
s\in G
, for suitable scalars
c_{t}
,

t\in G
, we have

\displaystyle W_{\lambda}(\pi(s)\otimes 1)\eta

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\in G}c_{t}W_{\lambda}(\pi(s)\otimes\rho(t))\psi

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\in G}c_{t}(\sigma_{A}^{i}(U_{s})\otimes\sigma_{B}^{j}(V_{t}))\xi_{i,j,k,l}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\in G}c_{t}(\sigma_{A}^{i}(U_{s})\otimes I_{H_{B}^{j}})(\sigma_{A}^{i}(U_{e})\otimes\sigma_{B}^{j}(V_{t}))\xi_{i,j,k,l}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\in G}c_{t}(\sigma_{A}^{i}(U_{s})\otimes I_{H_{B}^{j}})W_{\lambda}(1\otimes\rho(t))\psi

\displaystyle=(\sigma_{A}^{i}(U_{s})\otimes I_{H_{B}^{j}})W_{\lambda}\eta.

Thus,
W_{\lambda}(\pi(s)\otimes 1)=(\sigma_{A}^{i}(U_{s})\otimes I_{H_{B}^{j}})W_{\lambda}
,
s\in G
. Similarly,
W_{\lambda}(1\otimes\rho(t))=(I_{H_{A}^{i}}\otimes\sigma_{B}^{j}(V_{t}))W_{\lambda}
,
t\in G
, so that

W_{\lambda}(\pi(s)\otimes\rho(t))=(\sigma_{A}^{i}(U_{s})\otimes\sigma_{B}^{j}(V_{t}))W_{\lambda},\ \ \ s,t\in G.

Then the unitaries
\sigma_{A}^{i}(U_{s})\otimes\sigma_{B}^{j}(V_{t})
are respectively mapped to the unitaries
\pi(s)\otimes\rho(t)
under the unital completely positive map

\Phi_{\lambda}:\mathcal{B}(H_{A}^{i}\otimes H_{B}^{j})\to\mathcal{B}(H_{\pi}\otimes H_{\rho})
,
given by
\Phi_{\lambda}(T)=W_{\lambda}^{*}TW_{\lambda}
.
Thus, the unitaries
\sigma_{A}^{i}(U_{s})\otimes\sigma_{B}^{j}(V_{t})

belong to the multiplicative domain
\mathcal{M}
of
\Phi_{\lambda}
. Hence, by
[
13
, Proposition 1.5.7]
,
\mathcal{M}
contains the
C^{*}
-algebra generated by
\sigma_{A}^{i}(U_{s})\otimes\sigma_{B}^{j}(V_{t})
, which, by the irreducibility of
\sigma_{A}^{i}
and
\sigma_{B}^{j}
(and the definitions of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
), is equal to
\mathcal{B}(H_{A}^{i}\otimes H_{B}^{j})
. So
\Phi_{\lambda}:\mathcal{B}(H_{A}^{i}\otimes H_{B}^{j})\to\mathcal{B}(H_{\pi}\otimes H_{\rho})
is a unital
*
-homomorphism, necessarily injective by simplicity of
\mathcal{B}(H_{A}^{i}\otimes H_{B}^{j})
and surjective by irreducibility of
\pi
and
\rho
. Hence,
W_{\lambda}
is unitary.

Moreover, the local intertwining properties above show that
the maps
\sigma_{A}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}(H_{\pi})\otimes I_{H_{\rho}}

and
\sigma_{B}:\mathcal{B}\to I_{H_{\pi}}\otimes\mathcal{B}(H_{\rho})
, given by

\sigma_{A}(a)=W_{\lambda}^{*}(\sigma_{A}^{i}(a)\otimes I_{H_{B}^{J}})W_{\lambda}\ \mbox{ and }\ \sigma_{B}(b)=W_{\lambda}^{*}(I_{H_{A}^{i}}\otimes\sigma_{B}^{j}(b))W_{\lambda}

are irreducible representations of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, respectively, and are independent of
\lambda
. Clearly,
\sigma_{A}\otimes\sigma_{B}
is unitarily equivalent to each
\sigma_{A}^{i}\otimes\sigma_{B}^{j}
, (via
W_{\lambda}
) so we must have local unitary equivalence:
\sigma_{A}\cong\sigma_{A}^{i}
and
\sigma_{B}\cong\sigma_{B}^{j}
, say, via
W_{A}^{i}:H_{A}^{i}\to H_{\pi}
and
W_{B}^{j}:H_{B}^{j}\to H_{\varphi}
. Then
W_{A}^{i}\otimes W_{B}^{j}=\alpha_{i,j}W_{\lambda}^{*}
for some phase
\alpha_{i,j}\in\mathbb{T}
.

We now follow ideas from
[
44
, Theorem 4.12]
. Let

\Lambda_{A}=\{i:p_{i,j,k,l}>0\ \textnormal{for some}\ j,k,l\},

and define
\Lambda_{B}
similarly. Fix unit vectors
\eta_{A}\in H_{\pi}
and
\eta_{B}\in H_{\rho}
. For
i\notin\Lambda_{A}
, let

T_{A}^{i}:H_{A}^{i}\otimes K_{A}^{i}\to H_{\pi}\otimes H_{A}^{i}\otimes K_{A}^{i}
be given by

T_{A}^{i}(\eta)=\eta_{A}\otimes\eta
, and define

T_{B}^{j}:H_{B}^{j}\otimes K_{B}^{j}\to H_{\rho}\otimes H_{B}^{j}\otimes K_{B}^{j}
, for
j\notin\Lambda_{B}
, similarly. Finally, set

H_{A}^{\rm aux}:=\bigg(\bigoplus_{i\in\Lambda_{A}}K_{A}^{i}\bigg)\bigoplus\bigg(\bigoplus_{i\notin\Lambda_{A}}H_{i}^{A}\otimes K_{A}^{i}\bigg),

and define the isometry
T_{A}:H_{A}\to H_{\pi}\otimes H_{A}^{\rm aux}
by

T_{A}=\bigg(\bigg(\bigoplus_{i\in\Lambda_{A}}W_{A}^{i}\otimes I_{K_{A}^{i}}\bigg)\oplus\bigg(\bigoplus_{i\notin\Lambda_{A}}T_{A}^{i}\bigg)\bigg)\circ W_{A}.

Define
H_{B}^{\rm aux}
and
T_{B}:H_{B}\to H_{\rho}\otimes H_{B}^{\rm aux}
similarly,
and let

\xi^{\rm aux}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n_{A}}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n_{B}}\sum_{k=1}^{d_{A}^{i}}\sum_{l=1}^{d_{B}^{j}}\alpha_{i,j}\sqrt{p_{i,j,k,l}}e_{k}^{i}\otimes e_{l}^{j}\in H_{A}^{\rm aux}\otimes H_{B}^{\rm aux}.

By construction, for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
and
b\in\mathcal{B}
we have

(T_{A}\otimes T_{B})(a\otimes b)\xi=((\sigma_{A}(a)\otimes\sigma_{B}(b))\psi)\otimes\xi^{\rm aux}.

In particular,

\displaystyle(T_{A}\otimes T_{B})(E_{s,s^{\prime},g,g^{\prime}}\otimes F_{t,t^{\prime},h,h^{\prime}})\xi

\displaystyle=\delta_{s,g}\delta_{s^{\prime},g^{\prime}}\delta_{t,h}\delta_{t^{\prime},h^{\prime}}T_{A}\otimes T_{B}(U_{s}^{*}U_{s^{\prime}}\otimes V_{t}^{*}V_{t^{\prime}})\xi

\displaystyle=\delta_{s,g}\delta_{s^{\prime},g^{\prime}}\delta_{t,h}\delta_{t^{\prime},h^{\prime}}(\pi(s)^{*}\pi(s^{\prime})\otimes\rho(t)^{*}\rho(t^{\prime}))\psi\otimes\xi^{\rm aux}

\displaystyle=\delta_{s,g}\delta_{s^{\prime},g^{\prime}}\delta_{t,h}\delta_{t^{\prime},h^{\prime}}(\pi(s^{-1}s^{\prime})\otimes\rho(t^{-1}t^{\prime}))\psi\otimes\xi^{\rm aux}.

Thus, the model
(H_{\pi}\otimes H_{\rho},\{\widetilde{E}_{s,s^{\prime},g,g^{\prime}}\},\{\widetilde{F}_{t,t^{\prime},h,h^{\prime}}\},\psi)
is a self-test, as claimed.
∎

We now exhibit a class of examples satisfying the hypotheses of Proposition
5.19
.

Example 5.20
.

Let
G=S_{3}
, the symmetric group on 3 points.
S_{3}
has a two dimensional irreducible representation
\pi:S_{3}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
given by

\displaystyle\pi(e)=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},\thinspace\thinspace\pi(123)=\begin{bmatrix}e^{i2\pi/3}&0\\
0&e^{-i2\pi/3}\end{bmatrix},\thinspace\thinspace\pi(132)=\begin{bmatrix}e^{-i2\pi/3}&0\\
0&e^{i2\pi/3}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\pi(12)=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix},\thinspace\thinspace\pi(23)=\begin{bmatrix}0&e^{-i2\pi/3}\\
e^{i2\pi/3}&0\end{bmatrix},\thinspace\thinspace\pi(13)=\begin{bmatrix}0&e^{i2\pi/3}\\
e^{-i2\pi/3}&0\end{bmatrix}.

In the argument below, we will interpret the above matrices as rotations on the Bloch sphere. To that end, recall that the single qubit rotation operators

R_{x}(\theta)=e^{-i\frac{\theta}{2}\sigma_{x}},\ \ R_{y}(\theta)=e^{-i\frac{\theta}{2}\sigma_{y}},\ \ R_{z}(\theta)=e^{-i\frac{\theta}{2}\sigma_{z}},

induce rotations of angle
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
about the
x
,
y
, and
z
axes, respectively, where
\sigma_{x}
,

\sigma_{y}
and
\sigma_{z}
are the
2\times 2
Pauli matrices.
Then

\pi(123)=R_{z}(2\pi/3),\ \ \pi(132)=R_{z}(4\pi/3),\ \ \pi(12)=X=-iR_{x}(\pi).

If
\{e_{0},e_{1}\}
denotes the standard basis of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, for
\theta\in(0,\pi/2)\cup(\pi/2,\pi)
, we let

\{e_{\theta},f_{\theta}\}
denote the following
y
-rotated basis:

e_{\theta}=R_{y}(\theta)e_{0}=\begin{bmatrix}\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\\
\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\end{bmatrix},\ \ f_{\theta}=R_{y}(\theta)e_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}-\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\\
\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\end{bmatrix}.

Let
\psi:=\alpha e_{\theta}\otimes e_{\theta}+\beta f_{\theta}\otimes f_{\theta}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
for fixed
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
satisfying
|\alpha|^{2}+|\beta|^{2}=1
and
|\alpha|^{2}\in(0,1/2)
. Then
\psi
has full Schmidt rank so is marginally cyclic. We now show that

V:=\mathrm{span}\{(\pi(s)\otimes\pi(t))\psi\psi^{*}(\pi(s^{-1})\otimes\pi(t^{-1})):s,t\in S_{3}\}=\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}),

thereby obtaining a self-testing
\mathcal{Q}_{\rm q}
-model for the channel
\Theta(u)
, where
u(s,t)=\langle(\pi(s)\otimes\pi(t))\psi,\psi\rangle
, by Proposition
5.19
.

First, by irreducibility of
\pi
and the orthogonality relation (
[
51
, Theorem III.1.1]
),

\sum_{s\in S_{3}}\pi(s)(\cdot)\pi(s)^{*}=\tr(\cdot)1,

so that

\pi(s)\rho\pi(s)^{*}\otimes 1,\ 1\otimes\pi(t)\rho\pi(t)^{*}\in V,\ \ \ s,t\in G,

where
\rho
is the reduced density matrix of
\psi
:

\rho=(\id\otimes\tr)(\psi\psi^{*})=|\alpha|^{2}e_{\theta}e_{\theta}^{*}+|\beta|^{2}f_{\theta}f_{\theta}^{*}=(\tr\otimes\id)(\psi\psi^{*}).

Therefore, it suffices to show that

(56)

\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\pi(s)\rho\pi(s)^{*}:s\in S_{3}\}=M_{2}(\mathbb{C})_{sa}.

Recall that the space of trace-1 self-adjoint
2\times 2
matrices is affinely isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
via

(x,y,z)\Leftrightarrow\begin{bmatrix}1/2+z&x-iy\\
x+iy&1/2-z\end{bmatrix},

with the convex subset of density operators corresponding to the unit ball. In particular, we can visualize
\rho
as the vector
r_{\rho}=(x_{\rho},y_{\rho},z_{\rho})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\lVert\cdot\rVert\leq 1}
, which lies on the straight line between the points
r_{e_{\theta}e_{\theta}^{*}}
and
r_{f_{\theta}f_{\theta}^{*}}
on the boundary sphere. Both these latter points lie on the great circle in the
xz
-plane, and, since
\theta\in(0,\pi/2)\cup(\pi/2,\pi)
, are neither on the
z
-axis, nor the
xy
-plane. (Recall that
r_{e_{0}e_{0}^{*}}
and
r_{e_{1}e_{1}^{*}}
are the North and South poles, respectively.) Thus, the points in the unit ball associated to the rotated states

\displaystyle\pi(123)\rho\pi(123)^{*}

\displaystyle=R_{z}(2\pi/3)\rho R_{z}(2\pi/3)^{*},

\displaystyle\pi(132)\rho\pi(132)^{*}

\displaystyle=R_{z}(4\pi/3)\rho R_{z}(4\pi/3)^{*},

\displaystyle\pi(12)\rho\pi(12)^{*}

\displaystyle=R_{x}(\pi)\rho R_{x}(\pi)^{*}

are affinely independent in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, so their affine hull yields all hermitian matrices of trace 1, and the equality (
56
) follows.

6.
Connections with
C^{*}
-envelopes

Recall that, if
\mathcal{S}
is an operator system,
its
C^{*}
-envelope

C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{S})
is the unital C*-algebra, uniquely determined up to isomorphism by the following universal property: there is a unital complete order embedding
\iota:\mathcal{S}\to C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{S})
such that
C^{*}(\iota(\mathcal{S}))=C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{S})
, and for any
C^{*}
-algebra

\mathcal{A}
and unital complete order embedding
\varphi:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{A}
with
C^{*}(\varphi(\mathcal{S}))=\mathcal{A}
, there is a surjective
*
-homomorphism
\pi:\mathcal{A}\to C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{S})
such that
\pi\circ\varphi=\iota
.
In this penultimate section, we show that the examples in Sections
5.2
(PVM self-tests),
5.6
(quantum graph coloring) and
5.7
(Schur product channels) are all instances of a single phenomena: unique state extension across

\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B}\subseteq C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{S}_{A})\otimes_{\max}C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{S}_{B}),

for pertinent operator systems
\mathcal{S}_{A}
and
\mathcal{S}_{B}
where the latter
inclusion is valid.

By contrast, general abstract self-testing concerns unique state extensions across

\mathcal{S}_{A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{B}\subseteq C^{*}_{u}(\mathcal{S}_{A})\otimes_{\max}C^{*}_{u}(\mathcal{S}_{B}),

the tensor product of
universal
C*-covers.
Similar comparisons can be made when self-testing among models which factor through
C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{S}_{A})\otimes_{\max}C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{S}_{B})
.

The observations that follow rely mainly on the work
[
6
]
, but along the way we establish new dilation results for stochastic operator matrices.

6.1.
PVM’s

As mentioned in Remark
5.5
, PVM self-testing fits in our general framework, where the family of states
s:C^{*}_{u}(\mathcal{S}_{X,A})\otimes_{\max}C^{*}_{u}(\mathcal{S}_{Y,B})\to\mathbb{C}
to be self-tested is restricted to
those that factor through the quotient map

\pi_{A}\otimes\pi_{B}:C^{*}_{u}(\mathcal{S}_{X,A})\otimes_{\max}C^{*}_{u}(\mathcal{S}_{Y,B})\to\mathcal{A}_{X,A}\otimes_{\max}\mathcal{A}_{Y,B}.

Note that the set of such states is precisely

(\pi_{A}\otimes\pi_{B})^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{X,A}\otimes_{\max}\mathcal{A}_{Y,B}))\cong\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{X,A}\otimes_{\max}\mathcal{A}_{Y,B}).

It is well-known that

\mathcal{A}_{X,A}\cong\underbrace{\mathcal{D}_{A}\ast_{1}\cdots\ast_{1}\mathcal{D}_{A}}_{|X|\mbox{ \tiny times}}\cong C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{S}_{X,A}),

and similarly for
\mathcal{A}_{Y,B}
, (for the latter isomorphism, see e.g.
[
6
, Corollary 2.9]
). Thus, a state
f:\mathcal{S}_{X,A}\otimes_{c}\mathcal{S}_{Y,B}\to\mathbb{C}
has a unique extension to

(\pi_{A}\otimes\pi_{B})^{*}(\mathcal{S}(C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{S}_{X,A})\otimes_{\max}C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{S}_{Y,B}))

if and only if it extends uniquely across

\mathcal{S}_{X,A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{S}_{Y,B}\subseteq C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{S}_{X,A})\otimes_{\max}C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{S}_{Y,B}),

the above inclusion being valid by
[
6
, Lemma 3.10]
.

6.2.
Semi-classical SOM’s

Extending the setup of Subsection
5.6
, let

\mathcal{B}_{X,A}=\underbrace{M_{A}\ast_{1}\cdots\ast_{1}M_{A}}_{|X|\mbox{ times}}.

For each
x\in X
, write
\{\epsilon_{x,a,a^{\prime}}:a,a^{\prime}\in A\}

for the canonical matrix unit system of the
x
-th copy of
M_{A}
, and let

\mathcal{R}_{X,A}={\rm span}\{\epsilon_{x,a,a^{\prime}}:x\in X,a,a^{\prime}\in A\},

considered as an operator subsystem of
\mathcal{B}_{X,A}
. By
[
6
, Corollary 2.9]
, the
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathcal{B}_{X,A}
is universal for unital
*
-homomorphisms
M_{A}\to\mathcal{D}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{A}
, where
\mathcal{A}
is a unital
C^{*}
-algebra (see
[
6
, Definition 2.2]
), and the pair
(\mathcal{R}_{X,A},\mathcal{B}_{X,A})
satisfies the hypotheses of
[
6
, Theorem 3.8]
. Hence,
C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{R}_{X,A})=\mathcal{B}_{X,A}
, and from
[
6
, Lemma 3.10]
(or
[
47
, Lemma 2.8]
),

\mathcal{R}_{X,A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{R}_{Y,B}\subseteq C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{R}_{X,A})\otimes_{\rm max}C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{R}_{Y,B}).

Following
[
55
, §7.1]
, an SOM
E\in M_{X}\otimes M_{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}(H)
is called
semi-classical
if
E=\sum_{x\in X}\epsilon_{x,x}\otimes E_{x}
with
E_{x}\in(M_{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}(H))^{+}
and
\tr_{A}E_{x}=I_{H}
,
x\in X
.
In
[
55
, Theorem 7.5]
it was shown that semi-classical SOMs correspond to unital completely positive maps
\mathcal{R}_{X,A}\to\mathcal{B}(H)
. Moreover, the state space of
\mathcal{R}_{X,A}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{R}_{Y,A}
is affinely isomorphic to the set of classical-to-quantum no-signalling correlations
\Gamma:\mathcal{D}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{Y}\to M_{A}\otimes M_{B}

[
55
, Theorem 7.7]
.

In light of the previous paragraphs,
Corollary
5.17
means that the state
\tilde{s}_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}:\mathcal{R}_{4,2}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{R}_{4,2}\to\mathbb{C}
given by

\tilde{s}_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}(w)={\rm tr}_{2}((\pi_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}\cdot\pi_{\mathcal{K}_{4}})(w)),\ \ \ w\in\mathcal{R}_{4,2}\otimes_{\rm c}\mathcal{R}_{4,2},

has a unique extension to the finite-dimensional states of
C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{R}_{4,2})\otimes_{\rm max}C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{R}_{4,2})
. Similarly, Proposition
5.18
means that
\tilde{s}_{\mathcal{K}_{4}}
is a self-test for the class of finite-dimensional semi-classical SOM models which factor through
C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{R}_{4,2})\otimes_{\rm max}C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{R}_{4,2})
.

6.3.
Unistochastic Operator Matrices

It is known that the entries of any SOM
E=(E_{x,x^{\prime},a,a^{\prime}})\in M_{X}\otimes M_{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}(H)
can be represented as
E_{x,x^{\prime},a,a^{\prime}}=V_{a,x}^{*}V_{a^{\prime},x^{\prime}}
for an isometry

V:H^{X}\to K^{A}
, where
V=(V_{a,x})_{a,x}

[
55
, Theorem 3.1]
. When
X=A
, it is natural to study further
the unistochastic SOM’s (USOM’s), that is,
those SOM’s for which
V
can be taken unitary (see Section
5.7
).
In this subsection, we show that any SOM can be dilated to a USOM, thereby establishing a matricial version of Naimark dilation between POVM’s and PVM’s. Along the way, we connect these notions to
C^{*}
-envelopes of pertinent operator systems, as done in the previously in this section.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{X}
denote the universal
C^{*}
-algebra generated by the elements
u_{a,x}

a,x\in X
of a unitary matrix
u=(u_{a,x})_{a,x}
, commonly known as the Brown algebra
[
12
]
. It was shown in
[
25
, Proposition 2.3]
that if
S_{1}^{X}
is the dual operator space
(M_{X})^{*}
,
then the map
e_{a,x}\mapsto u_{a,x}
, from

S^{X}_{1}
into
\mathrm{span}\{u_{a,x}:a,x\in X\}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{X}

is a completely isometric isomorphism.

Recall from Subsection
5.1
the universal TRO of a block operator isometry
v=(v_{a,x})_{a,x\in X}

[
55
]
, hereby denoted simply
\mathcal{V}_{X}
as we assume
that
X=A
throughout this subsection; thus,
\mathcal{V}_{X}
is universal for the relations

(57)

\sum_{a\in X}v_{a,x}^{*}v_{a,x^{\prime}}=\delta_{x,x^{\prime}}1,\ \ \ x,x^{\prime}\in X,

in that every (concrete) block operator isometry
V=(v_{a,x})_{a,x}
,
whose entries lie in
\mathcal{B}(H,K)
for some Hilbert spaces
H
and
K
,
gives rise to a unique
ternary morphism
\theta_{V}:\mathcal{V}_{X}\to\mathcal{B}(H)
such that
\theta(v_{a,x})=V_{a,x}
,
x,a\in X
.
Since the entries of
u
satisfy the relations (
57
), there
exists a (unique) ternary morphism
\varphi:\mathcal{V}_{X}\to\mathcal{B}_{X}
such that
\varphi(v_{a,x})=u_{a,x}
. On the other hand, since
v=(v_{a,x})_{a,x}
belongs to the unit ball of

M_{X}(\mathcal{V}_{X})
, the canonical completely isometric identification
M_{X}(\mathcal{V}_{X})\cong\mathcal{CB}(S_{1}^{X},\mathcal{V}_{X})

(see e.g.
[
5
, Proposition 1.5.14]
),
yields a complete contraction
\varphi^{\prime}:\mathrm{span}\{u_{a,x}:a,x\in X\}\to\mathrm{span}\{v_{a,x}:a,x\in X\}
satisfying
\varphi^{\prime}(u_{a,x})=v_{a,x}
. Since ternary morphisms are completely contractive, it follows that the restriction

\kappa:\mathrm{span}\{u_{a,x}:a,x\in X\}\to\mathrm{span}\{v_{a,x}:a,x\in X\}

of
\varphi^{\prime}
is a complete isometry (see also
[
18
, Proposition 4.1]
). This first order isomorphism leads to the following dilation result.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
H
and
K
be Hilbert spaces, for which

(v_{a,x})_{a,x}\in M_{X}(\mathcal{B}(H,K))
.
There exists a Hilbert space
L
, isometries
w_{1}:H\to L
,
w_{2}:K\to L
and a unital *-homomorphism
\pi:\mathcal{B}_{X}\to\mathcal{B}(L)
satisfying

v_{a,x}=w_{2}^{*}\pi(u_{a,x})w_{1}\ \ \ \textnormal{and}\ \ w_{2}w_{2}^{*}\pi(u_{a,x})w_{1}=\pi(u_{a,x})w_{1}\ \ \ x,a\in X.

Proof.

By the injectivity of
\mathcal{B}(H,K)
, we can extend
\kappa
to a complete contraction
\widetilde{\kappa}:\mathcal{B}_{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(H,K)
. By the
Haagerup-Paulsen-Wittstock representation theorem for complete contractions, there exists a Hilbert space
L
, isometries
w_{1}:H\to L
,
w_{2}:K\to L
and a unital *-homomorphism
\pi:\mathcal{B}_{X}\to\mathcal{B}(L)
such that
\widetilde{\kappa}(a)=w_{2}^{*}\pi(a)w_{1}
,
a\in\mathcal{B}_{X}
. (We note that this
can be derived by appling the proof of
[
13
, Theorem B.7]
together with the rectangular version of Paulsen’s off-diagonal trick
[
5
, Lemma 1.3.15]

and refer to
[
24
, Theorem 2.1.12]
for a complete argument.)
In particular,

v_{a,x}=\kappa(u_{a,x})=w_{2}^{*}\pi(u_{a,x})w_{1},\ \ \ x,a\in X.

Then
v=(w_{2}^{*}\otimes I_{X})\pi_{X}(u)(w_{1}\otimes I_{X})
, where
\pi_{X}:=(\pi\otimes\id_{M_{X}})
. Since

(w_{2}\otimes I_{X})v=(w_{2}w_{2}^{*}\otimes I_{X})\pi_{X}(u)(w_{1}\otimes I_{X})

with both
(w_{2}\otimes I_{X})v
and
\pi_{X}(u)(w_{1}\otimes I_{X})
isometries, we have that

\pi_{X}(u)(w_{1}\otimes I_{X})H\otimes\mathbb{C}^{X}\subseteq\mathrm{ran}(w_{2}\otimes I_{X})
.
∎

Corollary 6.2
.

There exists a complete order isomorphism

\varphi:\mathcal{T}_{X}\to\mathrm{span}\{u_{a,x}^{*}u_{a^{\prime},x^{\prime}}:x,x^{\prime},a,a^{\prime}\in X\}
satisfying

(58)

\varphi(v_{a,x}^{*}v_{a^{\prime},x^{\prime}})=u_{a,x}^{*}u_{a^{\prime},x^{\prime}},\ \ \ x,x^{\prime},a,a^{\prime}\in X.

Proof.

By the universal property of

v=(v_{a,x})_{a,x}
, there exists a (non-degenerate) TRO morphism
\mathcal{V}_{X}\to\mathcal{B}_{X}
mapping
v_{a,x}
to
u_{a,x}
,
x,a\in X
.
The latter morphism induces a unital
*
-homomorphism
\varphi:\mathcal{C}_{X}\to\mathcal{B}_{X}
mapping
v_{a,x}^{*}v_{a^{\prime},x^{\prime}}
to
u_{a,x}^{*}u_{a^{\prime},x^{\prime}}
,

x,x^{\prime},a,a^{\prime}\in X
, where
\mathcal{C}_{X}:=\mathcal{C}_{X,X}
.
Thus
\varphi|_{\mathcal{T}_{X}}
is a unital completely positive map satisfying (
58
).

Represent
v
faithfully inside
M_{X}(\mathcal{B}(H,K))

for some Hilbert spaces
H
and
K
.
By Proposition
6.1
, there exists a Hilbert space
L
, isometries
w_{1}:H\to L
,
w_{2}:K\to L
and a unital *-homomorphism
\pi:\mathcal{B}_{X}\to\mathcal{B}(L)
satisfying

v_{a,x}=w_{2}^{*}\pi(u_{a,x})w_{1}\ \ \ \textnormal{and}\ \ w_{2}w_{2}^{*}\pi(u_{a,x})w_{1}=\pi(u_{a,x})w_{1},\ \ \ x,a\in X.

But then

v_{a,x}^{*}v_{a^{\prime},x^{\prime}}=w_{1}^{*}\pi(u_{a,x}^{*})w_{2}w_{2}^{*}\pi(u_{a^{\prime},x^{\prime}})w_{1}=w_{1}^{*}\pi(u_{a,x}^{*}u_{a^{\prime},x^{\prime}})w_{1},

for all
x,x^{\prime},a,a^{\prime}\in X
.
It follows that

w_{1}^{*}\pi(\cdot)w_{1}
is a unital completely positive inverse to
\varphi
.
∎

Let
E
be a unistochastic operator matrix acting on a
Hilbert space
H
. When
H=\mathbb{C}
, and
E
is diagonal in the sense that
E_{x,x^{\prime},a,a^{\prime}}=\delta_{x,x^{\prime}}\delta_{a,a^{\prime}}E_{x,x,a,a}
, we recover the usual notion of unistochastic matrices. A simple application of the previous results yields the following dilation property.

Corollary 6.3
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space and

E=(E_{x,x^{\prime},a,a^{\prime}})\in M_{X}\otimes M_{X}\otimes\mathcal{B}(H)
be a SOM. Then there
exists a Hilbert space
K
, an isometry
W:H\to K
and a
block operator unitary
(U_{a,x})_{a,x\in X}\in M_{X}\otimes\mathcal{B}(K)
,
such that
E_{x,x^{\prime},a,a^{\prime}}=W^{*}U_{a,x}^{*}U_{a^{\prime},x^{\prime}}W
.

Proof.

By the universal property of
\mathcal{T}_{X}
, there is unital completely positive map
\psi:\mathcal{T}_{X}\to\mathcal{B}(H)
satisfying
\psi(e_{x,x^{\prime},a,a^{\prime}})=E_{x,x^{\prime},a,a^{\prime}}
. Let
\varphi^{-1}:\mathrm{span}\{u_{a,x}^{*}u_{a^{\prime},x^{\prime}}:x,x^{\prime},a,a^{\prime}\in X\}\to\mathcal{T}_{X}
be the complete order isomorphism from Corollary
6.2
. Extending the unital completely positive map
\psi\circ\varphi^{-1}
to
\mathcal{B}_{X}\to\mathcal{B}(H)
(by injectivity of
\mathcal{B}(H)
) and appealing to a Stinespring representation of the extension yields the desired conclusion.
∎

Using results from
[
6
]
,
we now show that the
C^{*}
-envelope of
\mathcal{T}_{X}
is the
C*-subalgebra
C^{*}(\mathcal{U})
of
\mathcal{B}_{X}
, generated by the
operator system

\mathcal{U}={\rm span}\{u_{a,x}^{*}u_{a^{\prime},x^{\prime}}:x,x^{\prime},a,a^{\prime}\in X\}.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
X
be a finite set. Then

C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{T}_{X})=C^{*}(\mathcal{U})
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, a unital
*
-homomorphism between unital
C^{*}
-algebras will simply be called a morphism. Let
\mathcal{A}
be the unital
C^{*}
-algebra which is universal for morphisms
M_{X}\mapsto M_{X}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}

in the sense that
there exists a morphism
\alpha:M_{X}\to M_{X}\otimes\mathcal{A}

such that, for any unital
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathcal{B}
and morphism
\beta:M_{X}\to M_{X}\otimes\mathcal{B}
, there is a unique morphism
\Lambda:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
such that
\beta=(\id\otimes\Lambda)\alpha

(see
[
6
, Theorem 2.3]
). Viewing
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(H)
, there exists a unitary
w:\mathbb{C}^{X}\otimes H\to\mathbb{C}^{X}\otimes H
such that
\alpha(T)=w^{*}(T\otimes I_{H})w
,
T\in M_{X}
. Since
\alpha
has range in
M_{X}\otimes\mathcal{A}
, we have that
w_{a,x}^{*}w_{a^{\prime},x^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{A}
for all
x,x^{\prime},a,a^{\prime}\in X
. Moreover,
\mathcal{A}
is generated by
\{(\rho\otimes\id)\alpha(A):\rho\in M_{X}^{*},\ A\in M_{X}\}

[
6
, Theorem 2.3]
, so
\mathcal{A}=C^{*}(\{w_{a,x}^{*}w_{a^{\prime},x^{\prime}}:x,x^{\prime},a,a^{\prime}\in X\})
.

If
u=(u_{a,x})_{a,x}
is the generating matrix for
\mathcal{B}_{X}
,

M_{X}\ni T\mapsto u^{*}(T\otimes 1)u\in M_{X}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{X}

is a morphism, so the universal property of
\mathcal{A}
supplies a morphism
\Lambda:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}_{X}
satisfying
\Lambda(w_{a,x}^{*}w_{a^{\prime},x^{\prime}})=u_{a,x}^{*}u_{a^{\prime},x^{\prime}}
,
x,x^{\prime},a,a^{\prime}\in X
. Then
\Lambda(\mathcal{A})\subseteq C^{*}(\mathcal{U})
, necessarily.

By the universal property of
\mathcal{B}_{X}
, there is a morphism

\pi:\mathcal{B}_{X}\to\mathcal{B}(H)
such that
\pi(u_{a,x})=w_{a,x}
,
x,a\in X
. The restriction of
\pi
to

C^{*}(\mathcal{U})
is an inverse to
\Lambda
, so that

C^{*}(\mathcal{U})\cong\mathcal{A}
.
The desired conclusion then follows from
[
6
, Theorem 3.8]
.
∎

It follows from the proof of Proposition
6.4
that the operator system
\mathcal{T}_{X}
satisfies the universal property of
[
6
, Theorem 3.3]
for unital completely positive maps
M_{X}\to M_{X}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{X}
, so that
[
6
, Corollary 3.11]
implies

\mathcal{T}_{X}\otimes_{c}\mathcal{T}_{Y}\subseteq C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{T}_{X})\otimes_{\max}C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}).

Therefore, the self-testing examples from Proposition
5.19
give rise to finite-dimensional states
f:\mathcal{T}_{X}\otimes_{c}\mathcal{T}_{Y}\to\mathbb{C}
which extend uniquely to finite-dimensional states of
C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{T}_{X})\otimes_{\max}C^{*}_{e}(\mathcal{T}_{Y})
.

7.
Questions

The notion of an approximate dilation
\tilde{S}
of a model
S

over the pair
(\mathcal{S}_{X,A},\mathcal{S}_{Y,B})
was defined in

[
58
]
for the case of classes of quantum models,
and can be easily extended to classes of quantum
commuting models over
a finitary context
(\mathcal{S}_{A},\mathcal{S}_{B})
,
where
\mathcal{S}_{A}={\rm span}\{f_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
and

\mathcal{S}_{B}={\rm span}\{g_{j}\}_{j=1}^{l}
:
given
\delta>0
, a quantum commuting model

\tilde{S}=(\tilde{H},\tilde{\varphi}_{A},\tilde{\varphi}_{B}\tilde{\xi})
is said to
\delta
-dilate a quantum commuting
model
S=(H,\varphi_{A},\varphi_{B},\xi)
if there exist
an auxiliary system
H_{\rm aux}
and a unit vector

\xi_{\rm aux}\in H_{\rm aux}
, and
a local isometry
V:H\to\tilde{H}\otimes H_{\rm aux}

such that

V\phi_{A}(f_{i})\phi_{B}(g_{j})\xi\sim^{\delta}\tilde{\phi}_{A}(f_{i})\tilde{\phi}_{B}(g_{j})\tilde{\xi}\otimes\xi_{\rm aux},\ \ \ i\in[k],j\in[l].

We say that a correlation
p
of quantum commuting type
over
(\mathcal{S}_{A},\mathcal{S}_{B})
is a robust
quantum commuting self-test if there exists a model

\tilde{S}
of
p
such that for every
\epsilon>0

there exists
\delta>0
with the property that, whenever

p_{S}
is a correlation arising from a quantum commuting model

S
over
(\mathcal{S}_{A},\mathcal{S}_{B})
with
\|p_{S}-p\|_{1}<\epsilon
,
we have that
\tilde{S}
is a
\delta
-dilation of
S
.
We have the following implications for
a correlation
p
:

p\mbox{ is a robust self-test}\ \Rightarrow\ p\mbox{ is a weak self-test}\ \Rightarrow\ p\mbox{ is a self-test}.

These implications show that weak self-testing
is a natural concept to study, but suggest the following
initial question:

Question 7.1
.

Is every weak self-test a self-test?

If
S
is a centrally supported quantum POVM model for
p\in\mathcal{C}_{\rm q}
and
S_{r}
is the reduced model of
S
, then
S\preceq S_{r}
(see
[
44
, Lemma 4.2]
), which reduces the question of self-testing of
p
to the study of full rank models of
p
. The latter was important for establishing a number of self-testing results and allowed the application of representations of certain algebraic relations (see e.g.
[
52
]
for a self-test of the optimal strategy of CHSH game, and
[
38
]
for self-tests of some synchronous correlations). We therefore formulate the following:

Question 7.2
.

For general model
S=(_{\mathcal{A}}H_{\mathcal{B}},\varphi_{A},\varphi_{B},\xi)
,
is it true that
S\preceq S_{r}
?

We point out that, currently,
we do not see how the construction of local isometries for the dilation
S\preceq S_{r}
can be modified to give one in the non-tensor split case.

In the definition of self-testing, an assumption is made on
the existence of an auxiliary quantum system that ampliates the ideal
model for the self-test in question. What types of auxiliary systems
may arise is an interesting question in its own right. As an example,
we formulate the following:

Question 7.3
.

When applying our definition of self-testing to (quantum) no-signalling correlations of quantum type (e.g. for the classes
\mathcal{C}_{\rm q}
and

\mathcal{Q}_{\rm q}
), do the auxiliary bimodules arising from the dilation
pre-order automatically tensor factorise into quantum spacial systems?

CCF-2115071 and DMS-2154459. The third author was supported by the Swedish Research Council project grant 2023-04555 and GS Magnusons Fond MF2022-0006.
